━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

```text
   VELOCITY CROSS-SECTION - ARROW LENGTH ENCODES SPEED
   ===================================================================
   PLAN VIEW ACROSS THE GLACIER (looking down from above)
   valley wall |                                       | valley wall
               |--->                               <---|
               |------>                         <------|
               |---------->                 <----------|
               |--------------->       <---------------|
               |------------------- CENTRE ------------|   FASTEST at the
               |               (maximum velocity)      |   centre-line,
               |                                       |   slowest at the
               +---------------------------------------+   margins: DRAG

   LONG SECTION THROUGH THE ICE (looking from the side)
   surface  ======================================>  |  fast
            =====================================>   |
            ===================================>     |   RIGID ZONE above:
            ================================>        |   ice here does not
   ....................................................  deform much; it is
            =============================>           |   CARRIED, and it
            ==========================>              |   CRACKS -> CREVASSES
            ======================>                  |
            =================>                       |  PLASTIC ZONE below:
            ============>                            |  deforms continuously
            =======>                                 |  under its own weight
   bed      ===>                                     |
   /////////////////////////////////////////////////// bedrock
         ^                                      ^
         |                                      |
   WARM-BASED (temperate):               COLD-BASED (polar):
   meltwater at the bed, ice at the      ice FROZEN to the bed,
   pressure-melting point -> it SLIDES   no sliding, no plucking
   -> PLUCKING + ABRASION + rapid        -> very little erosion;
      erosion                               relief is PRESERVED
   ===================================================================
   TOTAL SURFACE VELOCITY = internal deformation + basal sliding
                            + deformation of a soft bed
```

Two sections, one encoding: wherever the arrow is longer, the ice is moving faster. The shape of those arrow fields explains the crevasse pattern above, the erosion pattern below and — at Lesson 12 and Lesson 14 — why two glaciers under the same climate can behave as though they were in different ranges.

### 1. Plain-language intuition before the terminology

⚠️ Put a block of cold toffee on a sloping tray. The top stays hard and brittle; the base, squeezed by the weight above, slowly spreads. Push the tray gently and the whole mass creeps downslope, cracking across the top as the lower part moves faster than the upper part can follow. That is a glacier. ❗ The two halves of the image are the two halves of the physics: **a brittle upper zone that cracks, carried on a plastic lower zone that flows.**

⚠️ The second intuition is about the bed. Slide the toffee on a dry tray and it grips; wet the tray and it slips. ❗ A glacier frozen to its bed grips and does almost no erosive work; a glacier with meltwater at its bed slips, and in slipping it plucks and abrades. That single difference is why Lesson 5's erosional engine must be preceded by this lesson rather than follow it.

### 2. The three components of motion

⚠️ Total surface velocity is the sum of up to three components, and naming all three — rather than only the first two — is what separates a textbook answer from a current one.

| Component | Mechanism | Where it dominates | Landform consequence |
|---|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ **Internal deformation (creep)** | Ice crystals glide along their basal planes under shear stress; deformation rate rises steeply with stress, so it is concentrated near the bed where the overburden is greatest | All glaciers; the *only* component in cold-based ice | Produces flow without erosion; preserves pre-existing relief |
| ⚠️ **Basal sliding** | A thin water film at the ice-rock interface, maintained by pressure melting and by geothermal and frictional heat, lets the ice slip; it moves past obstacles by **regelation** (melting on the upstream high-pressure side, refreezing on the downstream low-pressure side) and by **enhanced creep** around larger bumps | Warm-based or temperate glaciers | **The erosive component.** Plucking and abrasion both require relative motion between ice and bed |
| ⚠️ **Deformation of a soft bed** | Where the glacier rests on unconsolidated sediment rather than bedrock, the sediment itself shears and carries the ice on top of it | Ice margins on thick till, and parts of former ice-sheet beds | Drumlin formation, Lesson 8; rapid ice-stream flow |

❗ The examinable payoff: **erosion is not a property of ice but of sliding ice.** ⚠️ A candidate who writes "glaciers erode by plucking and abrasion" has stated the mechanisms; one who adds "both require basal sliding, which requires a bed at the pressure-melting point" has stated the condition — and the condition is what explains why vast areas of central Antarctica and northern Canada retain pre-glacial landscapes beneath kilometres of ice.

### 3. The velocity field, and the two things it produces

⚠️ **Across the glacier**, velocity is greatest at the centre-line and least at the margins, because the valley walls exert drag. ⚠️ **Through the depth**, velocity is greatest at the surface and declines downward, because internal deformation accumulates with depth; where basal sliding is present, the whole profile is shifted by a constant amount, since sliding moves the entire ice column together.

⚠️ Two important consequences follow, and both are visible on any glacier photograph.

- ⚠️ **Debris carried on the surface is sorted into flow-parallel lines** by the differential velocity field, which is how lateral moraines from two merging glaciers become a **medial moraine** down the centre of the combined trunk (Lesson 8).
- ⚠️ **The upper ice cracks.** Because the rigid upper zone cannot deform fast enough to match the plastic zone beneath, it fails in tension and opens **crevasses**. Crevasse orientation is therefore a *map of the stress field*, and three named cases matter.

| Feature | Where and why | Source |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Bergschrund** | *"A deep, tensional crevasse formed around the head of a cirque glacier."* — it opens where moving ice pulls away from the ice or rock frozen to the headwall | Majid Husain, printed p.2.33 (local scan PDF p.55) |
| ✅ **Icefall** | A steep step in the bed over which the glacier accelerates and breaks into a chaotic mass of blocks | Majid Husain, printed p.2.33 (local scan PDF p.55) |
| ✅ **Pinnacle or serac** | The ice towers between intersecting crevasses, characteristic of an icefall | Majid Husain, printed p.2.33 (local scan PDF p.55) |

⚠️ The bergschrund is worth holding carefully, because Lesson 6 uses it as the mechanical engine of cirque excavation: it provides the pathway by which meltwater reaches the headwall, freezes in joints and prises rock away.

### 4. Surging — the flow instability that makes "all glaciers retreat" false as stated

⚠️ Most glaciers flow at a rate that changes slowly. A minority do not: they alternate between long quiescent phases, during which ice accumulates in a reservoir area, and short **surge** phases lasting months to a few years, during which velocity increases by an order of magnitude or more and the ice is rapidly transferred down-glacier, often advancing the terminus substantially.

⚠️ The mechanism is a switch in the basal regime: a change in the subglacial drainage system from efficient channels to a distributed, high-pressure water film raises basal water pressure, reduces the effective pressure holding the ice against its bed, and allows sliding to dominate. ❗ A surge is therefore **not** a response to a favourable climate. A surging glacier can advance several kilometres during a warming decade, because the ice being delivered was accumulated earlier and the trigger is internal.

❗ The examinable consequence is large and is cashed at Lesson 14: **an advancing terminus is not evidence against regional glacier loss, and a surging glacier in an official inventory is not a counter-example to a warming signal.** ⚠️ Both statements are forms of the same discipline — separate the *mechanism* from the *forcing* before drawing an inference. 📰 Lesson 14 supplies the first-party Indian evidence that surge-type glaciers are numerous in one Himalayan sector and that one of them is recorded as having surged in an official annexure.

### 5. Why flow matters to everything downstream in this file

⚠️ Four later lessons depend on this one, and naming the dependency is how a candidate turns a mechanism into an argument.

1. **Lesson 5 and Lesson 6** — erosion requires sliding; sliding requires a thawed bed. Hence glacial erosion is **selective**, not uniform.
2. **Lesson 8** — the differential velocity field sorts surface debris into moraine geometry; the plastic lower zone carries the basal debris that does the abrading.
3. **Lesson 14** — the measurement of "glacier change" is confounded by flow: a slow glacier and a fast glacier with identical mass balances will show very different snout behaviour.
4. **Lesson 17** — ice buried within a moraine dam is ice that was delivered there by flow; its slow melt is what weakens the dam from inside.

### 6. 🇮🇳 India hook — a warm-based range with a cold-based margin, and the limitation

🇮🇳 ⚠️ The Himalayan ice relevant to India is overwhelmingly of the **valley and cirque** kind in terrain that is steep, seismically active and, in the monsoon-fed sectors, comparatively warm for its altitude. ⚠️ Glaciers in such settings are generally expected to be **warm-based or partly so in their lower reaches**, which is consistent with the vigorous erosional and depositional record the range displays: deep troughs, extensive lateral and terminal moraine complexes and heavy sediment delivery to the rivers. ✅ The Advanced owner's observation that **ancient moraines lie below present snouts** is itself evidence of a long history of erosive, sliding ice reaching far lower than it does today.

🇮🇳 ⚠️ By contrast the high, arid Trans-Himalayan and Karakoram interiors — ✅ which the Advanced owner records as carrying a **high, arid snowline** — are colder and drier, and some of their higher ice is plausibly cold-based or polythermal.

❗ **The limitation, stated with the hook, and it is a real one.** The thermal regime of Indian Himalayan glaciers is **not established by any source available to this edition**. No book in this lane reports bed temperatures, and none of the seven official documents consulted states a thermal classification. ❗ This edition therefore presents the warm-based inference as an ⚠️ inference consistent with the landform record, and **not** as a verified property. ⚠️ A candidate should do exactly the same: write "the erosional and depositional record implies vigorous basal sliding in the lower reaches", not "Himalayan glaciers are temperate".

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | What it explains | What it does not explain |
|---|---|---|
| Internal deformation | Why all glaciers flow, including those frozen to their beds, and why the velocity profile declines with depth | Why some glaciers erode deeply and others do not — that requires the basal component |
| Basal sliding and the thermal regime | Why erosion is selective, why troughs are deep in some ranges and absent in others, and why preserved pre-glacial surfaces exist beneath ice | The *rate* of erosion, which is not derivable from the regime alone |
| Surging | Why an advancing terminus is not evidence of a favourable climate | The trigger timing of any individual surge, which remains a subject of research |

**Strongest objection.** The thermal regime is a research distinction. An examination answer on Indian glaciers can be written without it, and introducing a warm-against-cold-based distinction risks an unverifiable claim about Himalayan ice.

**Reply.** The objection is half right and the correct response is to keep the distinction while refusing the unverifiable claim. ⚠️ The distinction earns its place because it **converts a list into a condition**: it is the reason glacial erosion is selective rather than universal, which is the single best explanation available for why glaciated uplands show scoured troughs beside untouched plateau surfaces. ❗ But the objection correctly identifies the risk, which is why this lesson states the Himalayan thermal regime as an inference from landform evidence and attaches the absence of a source explicitly. **Keep the mechanism; refuse the attribution.** That is the same discipline the whole of Arc B applies to numbers.

⚠️ **Residual.** The relative contributions of internal deformation, basal sliding and bed deformation to total velocity vary between glaciers and within a single glacier over a year, and are not derivable from surface observation alone. No partition is asserted anywhere in this file.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Salient features of world's physical geography" (Mains General Studies Paper I, direct ownership).
- **Verified linkage:** no owned demand sits here. The lesson is the enabling condition for the **2023** fjord demand, because over-deepening below sea level requires sustained basal erosion, and it supplies the mechanism that makes the surge evidence at Lesson 14 intelligible.
- **Probable framing:** flow appears in an answer as a **one-clause condition** attached to an erosion statement — "both processes require basal sliding, and therefore a bed at the pressure-melting point" — or as the explanation of a crevasse or icefall in a landform-identification question.
- **Traps:** asserting that all glaciers erode; treating crevasses as evidence of melting rather than of tension; calling a bergschrund an ordinary crevasse; reading an advancing terminus as a climate signal; asserting a thermal regime for Himalayan glaciers that no source supplies; confusing a serac with a nunatak.
- **Answer use:** the reusable clause is *"Glacial erosion requires relative motion at the bed, so it is concentrated where the ice is at the pressure-melting point and slides; where the ice is frozen to its bed it flows by internal deformation alone and preserves rather than destroys the pre-existing surface."*

Revision notes:

- ⚠️ **Three components of motion: internal deformation (creep) · basal sliding · deformation of a soft bed.** Surface velocity is their sum.
- ⚠️ **Internal deformation** = basal-plane glide within crystals; concentrated near the bed where stress is greatest; the only component in cold-based ice.
- ⚠️ **Basal sliding** requires a water film; moves past obstacles by **regelation** (melt on the upstream side, refreeze on the downstream side) and by enhanced creep.
- ❗ **Erosion is a property of sliding ice, not of ice.** Warm-based → plucking and abrasion; **cold-based → relief preserved**.
- ⚠️ **Velocity field:** fastest at the **centre-line** and at the **surface**; slowest at the **margins** and at the **bed** (drag and accumulated shear).
- ⚠️ Differential velocity **sorts surface debris into flow-parallel lines** → medial moraines (Lesson 8).
- ⚠️ The **rigid upper zone cracks in tension → crevasses**; crevasse pattern maps the stress field.
- ✅ **Bergschrund** = *"A deep, tensional crevasse formed around the head of a cirque glacier"*; ✅ **icefall** = a steep bed step with chaotic broken ice; ✅ **pinnacle / serac** = the ice towers between crevasses.
- ⚠️ **Surging:** long quiescence → short, order-of-magnitude velocity increase → rapid down-glacier mass transfer and terminus advance. Trigger is a **basal drainage switch**, internal, not climatic.
- ❗ Therefore an **advancing terminus is not evidence against regional loss** (first-party Indian evidence at Lesson 14).
- 🇮🇳 ⚠️ Himalayan lower reaches **inferred** warm-based from the erosional and moraine record; ✅ **ancient moraines below present snouts** support a long history of erosive ice far lower down.
- ❗ **No source in this lane states the thermal regime of any Indian glacier.** Write the inference as an inference.
- ⚠️ No partition of velocity between the three components is asserted for any glacier.

### Concept check

**Question:** Two ice masses of similar thickness rest on bedrock of similar hardness for a similar length of time. One leaves a deeply scoured landscape of troughs and rock basins; the other leaves a landscape whose pre-glacial weathered surface survives almost intact beneath the former ice. Explain the difference and state what it implies for how glacial erosion should be described.

**Model answer:** The difference is the **thermal regime at the bed**, and through it the presence or absence of basal sliding. Glacial erosion operates by plucking, in which ice freezes to jointed rock and prises blocks away, and by abrasion, in which debris embedded in the ice basal layer scratches and grinds the surface beneath. Both require **relative motion between the ice and its bed**. Where the bed is at the pressure-melting point, a thin water film is maintained by pressure melting and by geothermal and frictional heat, the ice slides, and the whole erosive machinery operates — producing troughs, rock basins and striated pavements. Where the bed is frozen to the rock, there is no sliding; the ice still flows, but it does so entirely by internal deformation within the column, with the basal ice essentially stationary relative to the rock. Such a glacier transports almost nothing from its bed and erodes almost nothing, so the pre-glacial surface, including weathered regolith, can survive beneath it. The implication for description is precise: **erosion is not a property of ice but of sliding ice**, so glacial erosion is **selective**, and a correct answer states the condition alongside the two processes rather than treating erosion as the automatic consequence of glaciation.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not assume that thicker or longer-lasting ice necessarily erodes more. Thickness raises the overburden pressure and therefore favours pressure melting at the bed, but the governing variable is whether the bed reaches the pressure-melting point at all, which depends on surface temperature, geothermal flux and ice thickness together — and a cold, thin, slow glacier in a continental interior can leave a landscape almost untouched.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "Glacial erosion is selective, not universal." Discuss, with reference to the mechanics of ice movement. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The statement is accurate, and its ground is flow mechanics. A glacier moves by internal deformation, by basal sliding, and where it rests on sediment by deformation of the bed itself. Only the second and third involve motion of the ice relative to its rock bed, and only such motion permits plucking, in which ice frozen into joints prises blocks away, and abrasion, in which entrained debris grinds the surface. Basal sliding requires a bed at the pressure-melting point, sustained by overburden, geothermal and frictional heat. Where it is absent, the glacier flows by creep alone and preserves the surface beneath it, including weathered material that an energetic agent would have removed. Selectivity follows at every scale: between regions, between the trunk and the interfluve, and between a scoured trough floor and an untouched plateau shoulder. The qualification is that the velocity partition cannot be inferred from surface observation alone.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for all three components of motion named and distinguished; 3 for basal sliding identified as the necessary condition for both erosional processes; 2 for the thermal-regime requirement stated as the condition for sliding; 1 for selectivity demonstrated at more than one scale; 1 for the unverifiable-partition qualification. Deduct 2 for presenting erosion as an automatic consequence of ice cover.

## Lesson 4 — The ice-mass typology

Progress: 4 / 19  |  Stage: Foundation  |  Subtopic: The seven-member family of ice masses with the scale that distinguishes them, the continental against valley contrast, the floating-against-grounded sea-level rule, and the ice-shelf buttressing argument

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Canonical owner span: Core owner §8.2 in full (L130-L144), comprising the seven-row ice-mass table, the ice-shelf buttressing clause and the boxed floating-against-grounded trap; §8.4 row 2 (L182) on the sea-level pathway.
Book span: Majid Husain, *Indian & World Geography*, printed p.2.33 (local scan PDF p.55) — the two glacier types, the Antarctic ice-sheet figures and the Greenland figures; G.C. Leong, Chapter 6, local-scan PDF pp.51-52 — the types of ice mass.
Current-affairs anchor: CA-4 — Ministry of Earth Sciences, Press Information Bureau release 2150828, 31 July 2025, used here only for the third of its three stated impact pathways, *rising sea level globally*, which is the official statement this lesson's rule qualifies.
Qdrant: unavailable (non-blocking).
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

| # | Ice mass | Form and setting | Scale | ⚠️ Diagnostic point an examiner can check |
|---:|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | ⚠️ **Cirque or corrie glacier** | Small body occupying an armchair hollow, often with no tongue | hundreds of metres to a few kilometres | The nursery form, and often **all that survives after retreat** — so its presence can record either an early or a late stage |
| 2 | ⚠️ **Valley or alpine glacier** | Confined between valley walls; flows down a pre-existing valley | a few to tens of kilometres | Produces the **trough, cirque, arête and horn** suite of Lessons 6 and 7 |
| 3 | ⚠️ **Ice field** | Interconnected ice filling an upland, but **flow still directed by the underlying relief** | tens to hundreds of kilometres | The intermediate case: the relief is buried but still steering the ice |
| 4 | ⚠️ **Ice cap** | Dome covering an upland, **flowing outward radially**, smaller in extent than a sheet | up to roughly 50,000 square kilometres by convention | **Buries the relief rather than following it** — the diagnostic contrast with an ice field |
| 5 | ⚠️ **Ice sheet** | Continental-scale ice cap | millions of square kilometres | Only **Greenland and Antarctica** today; the Pleistocene sheets created the landforms of northern Europe and North America |
| 6 | ⚠️ **Piedmont glacier** | Valley glaciers emerging onto a lowland at the mountain front and spreading into a lobe | tens of kilometres | Transitional between **confined and unconfined** flow |
| 7 | ⚠️ **Ice shelf (and the icebergs it calves)** | Floating extension of an ice sheet over the sea, fed by grounded ice behind it | hundreds of kilometres | **Already floating**, so its collapse does not itself raise sea level — but it **buttresses** the grounded ice behind, and losing that buttress accelerates discharge that does |

⚠️ Read the table down the scale column, not down the name column. ❗ The family is a continuum of **size and of the relationship between ice and relief**, and three of the four places an examiner can test it — ice field against ice cap, ice cap against ice sheet, and shelf against grounded ice — are decided by exactly those two variables.

### 1. The organising question, stated before the names

⚠️ A list of seven ice masses is worth very little; the question that generates the list is worth a great deal. That question is: **does the relief control the ice, or does the ice control itself?**

- ⚠️ At the small end, the relief is in charge. A cirque glacier sits where a hollow allows it; a valley glacier goes where the valley goes. Flow direction is **inherited**.
- ⚠️ In the middle, control is shared. An ice field submerges the lower relief but its flow is still steered by the ridges beneath.
- ⚠️ At the large end, the ice is in charge. An ice cap and an ice sheet build their own surface slope, and the ice flows **outward from the dome**, downslope on the ice surface, regardless of the bed topography — which may even slope the other way.

❗ That single axis predicts the landform consequence too. Relief-controlled ice **deepens and sharpens the existing relief** — troughs, arêtes, horns. Self-controlled ice **smooths and scours broadly** — the ice-scoured lowlands of the Canadian and Fennoscandian shields, and the drumlin fields of Lesson 8.

### 2. The two-type contrast, in the book's own figures

✅ Majid Husain, at printed p.2.33 (local scan PDF p.55), reduces the family to two types for teaching purposes and attaches scale figures to each.

| ✅ Type | ✅ Book-era figures | ⚠️ What the figures are for |
|---|---|---|
| **Continental** | The **Antarctic ice sheet averages about 2,000 metres thick over some 8 million square kilometres**; **Greenland reaches about 3,000 metres at the central dome over about 1.3 million square kilometres** | Establishes the order-of-magnitude gap between polar and mountain ice, and supports the Lesson 1 point that global ice is overwhelmingly polar |
| **Valley or mountain** | Named for the **Himalaya, the Rockies, the Andes and the Alps** | Establishes that India's entire ice estate belongs to the smaller of the two classes |

❗ Both rows are **book-era textbook figures** and are quoted as such, with the book named. ⚠️ They are used here as **scale anchors**, not as current measurements, and no trend, loss rate or sea-level equivalent is computed from them anywhere in this file.

### 3. The rule this topic owns, and almost everyone gets wrong

❗ **Melting of already-floating ice does not directly raise sea level. Transfer of grounded ice to the ocean does.**

⚠️ The physics in one sentence: a floating body already displaces its own weight of water, so when it melts it produces exactly the volume of water it was displacing, and the level is unchanged. ⚠️ Grounded ice is different: it is resting on rock above the water, so when it melts and the meltwater reaches the sea, mass is **added** to the ocean that was not previously in it.

| Ice | Grounded or floating? | Direct effect of its melt on sea level |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ Sea ice | Floating | **None directly** |
| ⚠️ Ice shelf | Floating | **None directly** |
| ⚠️ Iceberg already in the water | Floating | **None directly** |
| ⚠️ Valley glacier, ice cap, grounded parts of an ice sheet | Grounded | **Raises sea level** |

❗ The reason this is in Topic 06 at all, rather than in the coasts topic, is that the Core owner assigns the **ice-mass typology and the floating-against-grounded rule** here while assigning the sea-level **mechanism** and its coastal consequences elsewhere. 🔁 Relative sea level, submergent and emergent coasts and coastal regulation belong to the coastal-landforms topic and are not developed here; 🔁 Arctic and Antarctic regional climate, sea-ice extent and polar governance belong to the polar-climate topic, which is the verified owner of the 2021 General Studies Paper I demand on melting Arctic ice and Antarctic glaciers and of the 2026 General Studies Paper I demand on tundra regions. ❗ Both of those demands are named and routed, not answered, in this file.

### 4. The buttressing argument — why shelf loss matters even though it raises no water

⚠️ The floating rule produces an obvious objection: if shelf melt adds nothing, why does shelf loss matter? The Core owner answers it in a single clause, and the answer is the most analytically valuable thing in this lesson.

```text
   THE BUTTRESS, DRAWN ONCE
   ------------------------------------------------------------------
      inland ice (GROUNDED)        grounding line      ICE SHELF
                                        |             (FLOATING)
      \\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\\ |  ========================
       \\\\\\ ice flowing seaward \\\\\\ |  ====== sea water =======
   /////////////// bedrock ////////////  |  ~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~
                                        ^
                                        |
      The shelf is pinned at its sides and on sea-floor highs.
      That pinning pushes BACK against the grounded ice behind it.
      REMOVE THE SHELF -> the back-pressure goes -> the grounded ice
      behind ACCELERATES across the grounding line -> and THAT ice
      is grounded, so its transfer DOES raise sea level.
   ------------------------------------------------------------------
   ERGO: the shelf's sea-level significance is INDIRECT and MECHANICAL,
         not a matter of its own meltwater.
```

❗ Write it as a two-step argument, never as a one-line claim. ⚠️ "Ice-shelf collapse raises sea level" is wrong as stated; "ice-shelf collapse removes the buttress that restrains grounded ice, and the accelerated discharge of that grounded ice raises sea level" is right, and it demonstrates command of a distinction most answers miss.

### 5. 🇮🇳 India hook — the four absent types, and what India's absence from the table actually teaches

🇮🇳 ⚠️ Of the seven types above, India has representatives of **three**: cirque glaciers, valley glaciers and, marginally, small ice fields in the highest Karakoram and Zanskar massifs. ⚠️ It has **no ice cap, no ice sheet, no piedmont glacier and no ice shelf**, and it has no coastline in contact with grounded ice.

🇮🇳 📰 ✅ The Ministry of Earth Sciences, in its Rajya Sabha reply of **31 July 2025**, lists **three impact pathways** of Himalayan glacier change, and the third is **"(3) rising sea level at global scale."** ⚠️ This lesson is what allows that official statement to be used correctly. India's Himalayan glaciers are **grounded** ice, so their loss does contribute to global sea-level rise — but the contribution is a *global* one, additive to a global total dominated by the two polar ice sheets, and it is **not** the mechanism by which Indian coasts are most immediately affected.

❗ **The limitation, stated with the hook.** The sea-level pathway is the **weakest of the three** for an Indian Mains answer, and a candidate who leads with it has mis-ranked the argument. ⚠️ The Core owner's own answer spine orders effects **in descending order of certainty**: altered dry-season flow reliability first, expanding glacial lakes and outburst exposure second, slope instability and sediment flux third, and sea-level contribution last. ❗ Use the sea-level pathway as the **final, qualified clause** of a Lesson 15 answer, attribute it to the ministry, and route the coastal consequence rather than developing it.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | What it explains | What it does not explain |
|---|---|---|
| The relief-control axis | Why the seven types form one continuum, and why ice caps smooth while valley glaciers sharpen | The threshold area at which an ice field becomes an ice cap, which is conventional rather than physical |
| The floating-against-grounded rule | Why sea-ice loss and shelf collapse raise no water directly, and why glacier and ice-cap loss does | The *magnitude* of any contribution — no sea-level equivalent is computed anywhere in this file |
| The buttressing argument | Why shelf loss is nevertheless consequential | The rate of grounded-ice acceleration following shelf loss, which is a modelling question owned elsewhere |

**Strongest objection.** India has no ice sheet, no ice shelf and no polar territory in the table's sense. A seven-row typology is therefore mostly irrelevant to an Indian paper, and the lesson is inherited from a world textbook rather than earned.

**Reply.** Two of the seven rows do real work in an Indian answer, and the typology is what makes them safe to use. ⚠️ First, the **cirque-against-valley distinction** is the difference between a glacier that still has an accumulation basin and one that has been reduced to a hollow — a distinction Lesson 11 needs to explain why Himalayan glacier counts are so sensitive to the minimum mapped size. ⚠️ Second, the **floating-against-grounded rule** is what prevents the commonest error in a cryosphere answer, and the official sea-level pathway at Lesson 15 cannot be stated correctly without it. ❗ The remaining five rows are taught at the cost of a single table because the world-physical-geography clause this topic is mapped to requires them, and because an answer that cannot place Himalayan ice within the global family cannot assess its significance.

⚠️ **Residual.** The boundary between an ice field, an ice cap and an ice sheet is **conventional**, set by area thresholds that different authorities draw differently. ❗ No threshold is asserted as authoritative here; the physically meaningful statement is about whether flow is steered by the bed or by the ice surface.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Salient features of world's physical geography" (Mains General Studies Paper I, direct ownership). ⚠️ This lesson is the typological content the *ice-caps and their changes* demand family presupposes.
- **Verified linkage:** no owned demand. 🔁 The two adjacent demands that most resemble this material — **2021 General Studies Paper I Question 15** on melting Arctic ice and Antarctic glaciers and weather patterns, and **2026 General Studies Paper I Question 5** on tundra regions — are both **routed to the polar-climate topic** by the verified ledgers and are **not** answered here.
- **Probable framing:** the typology enters an answer as a **classifying sentence** that prevents a category error — "ice shelves and sea ice are already floating, so the sea-level contribution comes from grounded ice: glaciers, ice caps and the grounded parts of the ice sheets."
- **Traps:** merging glaciers, ice shelves and sea ice into one category; claiming that shelf collapse directly raises sea level; claiming that it is therefore unimportant; treating "ice cap" and "ice sheet" as synonyms; quoting the book-era Antarctic and Greenland figures as current; leading an Indian water answer with the sea-level pathway.
- **Answer use:** the reusable pair is *"Melting floating ice does not itself raise sea level; grounded ice transferred to the ocean does"* followed immediately by *"which is why ice-shelf loss matters mechanically — it removes the buttress restraining grounded ice behind it."*

Revision notes:

- ⚠️ **Seven ice masses:** cirque · valley/alpine · ice field · ice cap · ice sheet · piedmont · ice shelf (with its icebergs).
- ⚠️ **Organising axis: does the relief steer the ice, or does the ice steer itself?** Relief-controlled ice **sharpens** relief; self-controlled ice **smooths and scours**.
- ⚠️ **Ice field** = relief buried but still steering flow; **ice cap** = dome flowing **radially outward**, burying relief; **ice sheet** = continental-scale cap, today **only Greenland and Antarctica**.
- ✅ Book-era scale (Majid Husain, printed p.2.33): **Antarctic ice sheet ~2,000 m thick over ~8 million km²**; **Greenland ~3,000 m at the central dome over ~1.3 million km²**.
- ✅ Valley or mountain type named for the **Himalaya, Rockies, Andes and Alps** — India's entire estate.
- ❗ **Floating ice melt ⇒ no direct sea-level rise.** Sea ice, ice shelves and icebergs are floating; valley glaciers, ice caps and grounded ice-sheet parts are **grounded**.
- ❗ **Buttressing:** shelf loss removes back-pressure → **grounded** ice accelerates across the grounding line → *that* transfer raises sea level. Two steps, never one.
- 🔁 Sea-level mechanism and coasts → coastal-landforms topic. 🔁 Arctic and Antarctic regional climate, sea ice, tundra and polar governance → polar-climate topic, the verified owner of the 2021 and 2026 demands named above.
- 🇮🇳 India has **three** of the seven types; **no ice cap, no ice sheet, no piedmont glacier, no ice shelf**.
- 📰 ✅ Ministry of Earth Sciences, 31 July 2025: three impact pathways — **runoff seasonality and variability · lake formation and GLOF frequency · rising sea level globally**.
- ❗ Rank the pathways by certainty: **flow reliability → lake growth and outburst exposure → slope instability and sediment → sea level last**, and attribute the last one.
- ⚠️ The ice-field / ice-cap / ice-sheet boundaries are **conventional area thresholds**; ❗ no threshold is asserted here.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "The Antarctic ice shelves are collapsing, which is directly raising global sea level and will inundate Indian coastal cities; meanwhile Arctic sea ice is melting, adding still more water." Correct the statement and explain what the corrected version does and does not establish.

**Model answer:** Both clauses contain the same error. **Ice shelves and sea ice are already floating**, and a floating body displaces its own weight of water; when it melts it yields precisely the volume it was displacing, so the level is unchanged. Neither shelf melt nor sea-ice melt therefore raises sea level **directly**. The corrected version of the first clause is a two-step mechanical argument: an ice shelf is pinned at its margins and on sea-floor highs, and that pinning exerts back-pressure on the grounded ice flowing towards it; losing the shelf removes the buttress, the grounded ice behind accelerates across the grounding line, and **the transfer of that grounded ice** raises sea level. The second clause has no such rescue — sea-ice loss has important consequences for albedo, ocean heat uptake and regional climate, but it is not a sea-level mechanism, and those consequences belong to the polar-climate owner rather than to this topic. What the corrected version establishes is that the sea-level contribution comes from **grounded ice**: glaciers, ice caps and the grounded parts of the ice sheets, including India's own Himalayan glaciers. What it does not establish is any magnitude, rate or coastal outcome. No quantity is derivable from the mechanism alone, and the coastal consequence belongs to the coastal-landforms owner.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not conclude from the floating rule that ice-shelf loss is unimportant. The rule denies a *direct* contribution and the buttressing argument restores an *indirect* one — and a candidate who states only the first half has replaced one half-truth with another.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** Distinguish the members of the ice-mass family and explain why the distinction matters for assessing the effects of ice loss. *(Distinguish and explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The family is ordered by scale and by whether the relief steers the ice. Cirque and valley glaciers are relief-controlled, sharpening relief into hollows, troughs and ridges. An ice field buries the lower relief while still being steered by it. An ice cap builds its own dome and flows radially outward, burying the relief; an ice sheet is its continental form, today only in Greenland and Antarctica. A piedmont glacier is the unconfined lobe where valley ice reaches a lowland; an ice shelf is a sheet's floating seaward extension. The distinction matters because the effect of loss depends on the member lost. Melting floating ice — shelves, icebergs, sea ice — raises sea level not at all; grounded glaciers, caps and sheet interiors do. Shelf loss nevertheless matters indirectly, by removing the buttress restraining grounded ice. The qualification is that the area thresholds separating field, cap and sheet are conventional.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 2 for the scale-and-relief axis used as the organising principle rather than a list; 2 for ice field, ice cap and ice sheet correctly distinguished; 2 for the floating-against-grounded rule stated correctly; 3 for the buttressing argument given as two steps; 1 for the conventional-threshold qualification. Deduct 2 for asserting that shelf collapse directly raises sea level and 2 for concluding that shelf loss is therefore unimportant.

## Lesson 5 — The erosional engine

Progress: 5 / 19  |  Stage: Core  |  Subtopic: Plucking and abrasion, the role of subglacial meltwater and pressure melting, frost shattering above the ice, the diagnostic marks each process leaves, and why glacial erosion is selective

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Canonical owner span: Core owner §1 (L15-L19) on plucking and abrasion and the roche moutonnée as a hallmark; §2 rows 1 and 6 (L35, L40); §4 must-know facts (L55-L58); the revision-chart discrimination row recording glacier work as plucking and abrasion with its erosional and depositional landform sets.
Book span: Majid Husain, *Indian & World Geography*, printed p.2.34 (local scan PDF p.56) — the erosional glossary including roche moutonnée as *"the hallmark of glacial erosion"*, crag-and-tail, and the cirque excavation mechanism; G.C. Leong, Chapter 6, local-scan PDF pp.52-53 — the erosional work of ice.
Current-affairs anchor: no genuine official anchor applies to this lesson. Erosional mechanics are not the subject of any of the seven verified official documents in this lane, and manufacturing a link to a glacier-retreat press release would attach a dated statistic to a process that does not need one.
Qdrant: unavailable (non-blocking).
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

```text
   PROCESS ON THE LEFT -- THE MARK IT LEAVES ON THE RIGHT
   =========================================================================
   PROCESS                            |  DIAGNOSTIC EVIDENCE ON THE GROUND
   -----------------------------------+-------------------------------------
                                      |
   FROST SHATTERING above the ice     |  ANGULAR, JAGGED summits standing
     water in joints freezes,         |  above smooth, rounded, ice-moulded
     expands, prises blocks apart     |  surfaces below
     (a WEATHERING process - see      |  -> the NUNATAK contrast (L6)
      the weathering owner)           |  -> supraglacial debris supply
            |                         |
            v                         |
   the loosened blocks fall onto      |  LATERAL and MEDIAL moraines (L8)
   the ice surface                    |
   -----------------------------------+-------------------------------------
                                      |
   PLUCKING / QUARRYING               |  STEEP, JAGGED, FRACTURED down-ice
     meltwater enters joints at the   |  faces
     bed, refreezes, bonds ice to     |  -> the lee side of a ROCHE
     rock; the moving ice drags the   |     MOUTONNEE
     block away                       |  -> cirque headwall and BACKWALL
     REQUIRES: jointed rock +         |  -> the deep end of a rock basin
     basal sliding (Lesson 3)         |
   -----------------------------------+-------------------------------------
                                      |
   ABRASION                           |  SMOOTH, POLISHED, SCRATCHED
     debris frozen into the basal     |  surfaces
     ice is dragged across the bed    |  -> STRIATIONS (fine scratches,
     like a file                      |     parallel to ice flow)
     REQUIRES: tools in the ice +     |  -> GROOVES (large) and polish (fine)
     basal sliding + enough normal    |  -> the gentle up-ice face of a
     pressure to hold the tools       |     ROCHE MOUTONNEE
   -----------------------------------+-------------------------------------
                                      |
   SUBGLACIAL MELTWATER               |  POTHOLES, channels cut UPHILL
     under pressure; can flow uphill  |  (because the driving gradient is
     where the ice-surface gradient   |   the ICE SURFACE, not the bed)
     demands it                       |  -> smoothed, fluted bedrock
   -----------------------------------+-------------------------------------
                                      |
   PRESSURE MELTING and REGELATION    |  the mechanism BEHIND the
     melt on the high-pressure        |  roche moutonnee asymmetry:
     up-ice side of a bump,           |  ABRADE up-ice, PLUCK down-ice
     refreeze on the low-pressure     |  ON THE SAME BUMP
     down-ice side                    |
   =========================================================================
   SELECTIVITY RULE: no basal sliding -> no plucking, no abrasion
                     -> relief is PRESERVED, not destroyed
```

Five processes, five signatures. ❗ Learn the right-hand column as hard as the left: an examiner can ask what a landscape *shows*, and the evidence column is what converts a process list into a reading of the ground.

### 1. The intuition before the terminology

⚠️ Imagine dragging a heavy sheet of frozen mud across a cobbled floor. Two different things happen at once. Loose cobbles that the frozen sheet grips are **lifted out and carried away**; the cobbles that stay put are **scratched and polished** by the grit embedded in the sheet's underside. ❗ Those are plucking and abrasion, and they are complementary rather than alternative: the first removes material in blocks, the second wears material away in fragments.

⚠️ The complementarity is the examinable point, because the two processes need **opposite conditions on the same bump** — plucking needs low pressure and open joints on the lee side, abrasion needs high pressure and tools on the stoss side. ❗ A single landform records both, and that landform is the one Majid Husain calls the hallmark.

### 2. Plucking, stated with its requirements

✅ The Core owner defines glacial erosion as **plucking (freeze-thaw prising rock) and abrasion (scratching and grinding with embedded debris)**. ⚠️ Plucking, also called quarrying, works as follows.

1. ⚠️ Meltwater at the bed, produced by pressure melting and by frictional and geothermal heat, penetrates **joints, bedding planes and fractures** in the bedrock.
2. ⚠️ On the **down-ice (lee) side** of an obstacle the pressure in the ice is lower, so the water **refreezes**, bonding the ice firmly to blocks of rock.
3. ⚠️ The glacier continues to move, and the frozen bond **drags the block out** of its socket.
4. ⚠️ The block is incorporated into the basal ice and becomes a **tool for abrasion** further down-glacier.

❗ Two conditions are necessary and both are examinable. **The rock must be jointed** — a massive, unjointed rock resists plucking almost completely — and **the ice must be sliding**, which returns to Lesson 3's thermal regime. ⚠️ This is why glacial erosion follows geological structure so closely: faults, joint sets and bedding weaknesses are the lines along which the ice can get a grip, and a glacier exploits them exactly as a river exploits a fault-guided course.

### 3. Abrasion, and the evidence it leaves

⚠️ Abrasion is the grinding of the bed by rock fragments held in the basal ice. The glacier acts as a file whose teeth are the entrained debris; the ice itself, being far softer than most bedrock, does almost no abrading on its own.

| ⚠️ Scale of mark | Produced by | What it tells the reader |
|---|---|---|
| **Polish** | Fine rock flour | The bed was abraded by ice carrying very fine tools |
| **Striations** | Sand- to pebble-sized tools dragged across the rock | ❗ **Direction of ice flow** — striations are parallel to it, and they are the most reliable field indicator of former flow direction |
| **Grooves** | Large boulders held in the basal ice | Sustained flow in one direction with a large tool |
| **Rock flour** | The ground-up product itself | The characteristic milky turbidity of a glacier-fed stream |

⚠️ Abrasion has its own requirements: **tools must be present**, the ice must press them against the bed with enough **normal force**, and the debris must be **renewed**, because tools become blunt and rounded. ❗ Too much normal pressure, and the ice can no longer slide past the bump; too little, and the tools are not held. Abrasion is therefore most effective at intermediate pressures, which is one reason glacial erosion is patchy even beneath sliding ice.

### 4. Frost shattering above the ice — owned elsewhere, used here

⚠️ Above the glacier surface, the rock walls are exposed to repeated freezing and thawing of water in joints. The expansion on freezing prises blocks apart, and gravity delivers them onto the ice surface.

🔁 **Frost weathering as a weathering process, and the mechanics of the slope failures that deliver large volumes of rock to a glacier surface, belong to the weathering, mass-movement and groundwater owner.** ❗ This topic takes exactly two things from that process and stops: it is the **supply mechanism for supraglacial debris**, which Lesson 8 turns into lateral and medial moraines and Lesson 2 turned into the debris-cover double effect; and it is the reason **nunataks and arêtes are angular while the surfaces below them are smooth** — the contrast Lesson 6 uses as a field diagnostic.

### 5. Roche moutonnée — one landform, both processes, and the reason it is the hallmark

✅ Majid Husain describes the roche moutonnée as an **asymmetric bump, one side ice-moulded and the other steepened or cliffed**, and calls it **"the hallmark of glacial erosion."**

```text
   ONE BUMP, TWO PROCESSES, ONE DIRECTION
   ------------------------------------------------------------
      ICE FLOW  ------------------------------------>

                        ..............
                 .......              \
          .......                      \
   ......                               \____
   ----------------------------------------------
     STOSS SIDE (up-ice)           LEE SIDE (down-ice)
     gentle | smoothed | polished  steep | jagged | fractured
     STRIATED                      PLUCKED
     high pressure -> abrasion     low pressure -> refreezing
                                   -> block removal
   ------------------------------------------------------------
   READ IT AS A COMPASS: the gentle, striated side faces UP-ICE.
   The landform records the DIRECTION of a glacier that has gone.
```

❗ It is the hallmark for a precise reason: it is **unambiguous**. ⚠️ A U-shaped valley can be approximated by other processes in unusual circumstances; a smoothed surface could be fluvial; but an asymmetric bump that is polished and striated on one side and plucked on the other, with the two sides consistently oriented across a whole region, can only have been produced by ice moving in one direction. ⚠️ It is also a **directional** indicator, which means it does work that no inventory of names can do: it lets a field observer reconstruct a vanished glacier's flow.

✅ The related form is the **crag-and-tail**, listed in the same glossary: a resistant mass protects softer material in its lee, which survives as a tapering ridge. ⚠️ The same asymmetry, at a larger scale and with a depositional rather than purely erosional tail.

### 6. Why glacial erosion is selective, and what the selectivity produces

⚠️ Pull the conditions together and the conclusion is forced. Erosion requires **sliding** (Lesson 3), **joints** for plucking, **tools and intermediate pressure** for abrasion, and **sustained flow in one direction**. Where any of those fails, erosion is weak or absent.

❗ Three consequences, each worth a clause in an answer.

1. ⚠️ **Erosion concentrates in the trunk.** The thickest, fastest ice is in the main valley, so troughs deepen while interfluves and plateau shoulders are barely touched — the geometry that produces **hanging valleys** at Lesson 7.
2. ⚠️ **Erosion can over-deepen.** Because the ice above presses the base down and the driving gradient is the ice surface rather than the bed, a glacier can excavate a **closed rock basin** and continue past it. A river cannot. ❗ This is the single mechanical fact that the 2023 fjord demand turns on, and Lesson 7 derives it fully.
3. ⚠️ **Erosion is structurally guided.** Troughs follow pre-existing valleys, faults and weak rock belts, which is why a glaciated landscape often looks like an exaggerated version of the landscape that preceded it.

### 7. 🇮🇳 India hook — the sediment the Himalaya delivers, and the limitation on reading it

🇮🇳 ⚠️ The clearest everyday evidence of the engine in India is the **rock flour** that gives glacier-fed Himalayan headstreams their characteristic milky grey colour in the melt season, and the enormous **sediment load** the Himalayan rivers carry out of the mountains. ⚠️ Abrasion is the manufacturing process for that flour, and the freshly exposed moraine left by a retreating snout is an additional, easily mobilised source — a link the Core owner records explicitly as **increased sediment and hazard flux from newly exposed moraine**.

❗ **The limitation, stated with the hook.** ⚠️ Himalayan sediment yield is **not** a measure of glacial erosion, and a candidate who treats it as one has overclaimed. 🔁 The load in a Himalayan river is the combined product of glacial abrasion, frost weathering, landslides and debris flows on tectonically steepened slopes, monsoon-driven fluvial erosion, and human disturbance — and the first belongs here while the second belongs to the weathering owner, the third partly to the weathering owner and partly to the disaster-management owner, and the fourth and fifth to the running-water owner. ❗ Say which component you mean, or say that the components cannot be separated from the bulk figure. ⚠️ That restraint is itself a mark-earning move, because it demonstrates the refusal of single-cause attribution that this topic's governing instruction demands.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| Pair | The discriminator | The wrong answer it prevents |
|---|---|---|
| **Plucking / abrasion** | **Block removal along joints** against **wearing by entrained tools** | "Glaciers erode by scraping" — which names only half the engine |
| **Striation / groove** | **Scale of the tool**, not a different process | Treating them as unrelated features |
| **Roche moutonnée / crag-and-tail** | The tail of the second is **protected material**, often partly depositional; the lee of the first is **plucked rock** | Using the two names interchangeably |
| **Frost shattering / plucking** | **Above the ice, by weathering** against **at the bed, by the glacier itself** | Attributing all angular debris to glacial erosion |

**Strongest objection.** Plucking and abrasion are two words that any candidate can memorise in thirty seconds. Devoting a lesson to them, with conditions and diagnostics, is disproportionate.

**Reply.** The two words are cheap; the **conditions** and the **diagnostics** are not, and they are what the better questions test. ⚠️ Conditions convert a description into an explanation: once a candidate can say that both processes require basal sliding, the selectivity of glacial erosion becomes derivable, and with it the hanging valley, the preserved plateau shoulder and the over-deepened basin. ⚠️ Diagnostics convert an answer into evidence: a candidate who can say that striations give flow direction and that a roche moutonnée's gentle side faces up-ice can *read* a landscape rather than list it, which is exactly what a landform-identification question rewards. ❗ And the over-deepening consequence is not optional — it is the mechanical premise of an owned ten-mark demand.

⚠️ **Residual.** The relative contribution of plucking and abrasion to total glacial erosion is **not fixed**; it varies with rock type, jointing density, debris supply, ice velocity and basal thermal conditions. ❗ No partition is asserted here, and a candidate should not assert one either.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Salient features of world's physical geography" (Mains General Studies Paper I, direct ownership).
- **Verified linkage:** this lesson is the **mechanical premise of the 2023 General Studies Paper I ten-mark demand** on the formation of fjords, because the *how* half of that demand is an over-deepening argument, and over-deepening is a consequence of the conditions established here. It also supports the standing directive the Core owner records as *"Distinguish glacial erosional and depositional landforms"*.
- **Probable framing:** erosion mechanics appear as the **second sentence** of any glacial-landform answer, immediately after the agent is named, and as the **evidence clause** in an identification question.
- **Traps:** naming only abrasion; omitting the sliding condition; treating frost shattering as a glacial process rather than a weathering process delivering debris; claiming that striations indicate the *amount* of erosion rather than its direction; reading a roche moutonnée's steep side as up-ice; using Himalayan sediment load as a measure of glacial erosion.
- **Answer use:** the reusable sentence is *"Ice erodes by plucking, in which refreezing bonds the ice to jointed rock on the low-pressure lee side and the moving glacier drags the block away, and by abrasion, in which entrained debris files the bed — and because both require basal sliding, glacial erosion is selective and structurally guided rather than uniform."*

Revision notes:

- ✅ **Glacial erosion = plucking + abrasion** (Core owner).
- ⚠️ **Plucking / quarrying:** meltwater enters joints → **refreezes on the low-pressure lee side** → bonds ice to rock → the block is dragged out. ❗ Requires **jointed rock** and **basal sliding**.
- ⚠️ **Abrasion:** entrained debris files the bed. ❗ Requires **tools**, **normal pressure** and **tool renewal**. The ice alone abrades almost nothing.
- ⚠️ **Marks: polish (fine flour) · striations (flow DIRECTION) · grooves (large tools) · rock flour** (the milky stream).
- 🔁 **Frost shattering is a weathering process** owned by the weathering and mass-movement topic. Here it is only the **supraglacial debris supply** and the reason **summits are angular while surfaces below are smooth**.
- ✅ **Roche moutonnée** = asymmetric bump, **one side ice-moulded, the other steepened or cliffed** — *"the hallmark of glacial erosion"*.
- ❗ **Read it as a compass: gentle, polished, striated side faces UP-ICE; steep, plucked side faces DOWN-ICE.**
- ⚠️ **Pressure melting and regelation** explain how one bump gets both treatments: melt on the high-pressure stoss side, refreeze on the low-pressure lee side.
- ✅ **Crag-and-tail** = resistant mass protecting softer material in its lee.
- ⚠️ **Subglacial meltwater is under pressure** and can run **uphill**, because the driving gradient is the **ice surface**, not the bed.
- ❗ **Selectivity:** no sliding → no erosion → **relief preserved**. Erosion concentrates in the trunk, can **over-deepen closed basins** (which a river cannot), and follows **structure**.
- 🇮🇳 **Rock flour** colours glacier-fed Himalayan headstreams; freshly exposed moraine adds easily mobilised sediment.
- ❗ Limitation: Himalayan **sediment yield is not a measure of glacial erosion** — glacial, weathering, mass-movement, fluvial and anthropogenic components cannot be separated from a bulk figure.
- ⚠️ **No partition** between plucking and abrasion is asserted; it varies with rock, jointing, tools, velocity and thermal regime.

### Concept check

**Question:** A field party reports from a deglaciated Himalayan valley: polished and finely scratched bedrock on the valley floor, all scratches running in one direction; a series of asymmetric rock bumps whose gentle sides all face up-valley; angular, frost-shattered rock debris on the ridge crests above; and a milky stream draining the present snout. Reconstruct what each observation establishes, and say what the set together cannot establish.

**Model answer:** Each observation reports a different process. The **polish and fine scratches** establish abrasion: debris entrained in the basal ice has been dragged across the bed under enough normal pressure to hold the tools against the rock. Because striations are cut parallel to the direction of movement, their common orientation establishes a **single, sustained flow direction**. The **asymmetric bumps** are roches moutonnées, and they corroborate the direction independently: the gentle, smoothed, striated stoss side faces up-ice and the steep, fractured lee side faces down-ice, because high pressure on the up-ice side favours abrasion while reduced pressure on the lee side allows refreezing, bonding and block removal. Their presence also establishes that the ice was **sliding at the bed**, since neither process operates in ice frozen to its bed. The **angular ridge-crest debris** establishes a process acting above the ice, frost shattering, which is weathering rather than glacial erosion and which supplied the supraglacial debris that became lateral and medial moraines. The **milky stream** establishes continuing abrasion at the present bed, since rock flour is the ground product. What the set cannot establish is **when the ice occupied the valley, for how long, how thick it was, or how much rock was removed**. Landforms record that ice was present and in which direction it moved; they do not date themselves or quantify the erosion without separate dating and volumetric evidence.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not read the angular ridge-crest debris as evidence of glacial erosion on the ridge. Its angularity is the signature of freeze-thaw weathering on rock standing *above* the ice, and the contrast between those angular crests and the smoothed surfaces below is itself the diagnostic of where the ice surface once stood.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "Glacial erosion exaggerates a landscape rather than replacing it." Examine. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The proposition holds because every erosional process ice uses is conditional. Plucking requires jointed rock and basal sliding; abrasion requires entrained tools, adequate normal pressure and renewal of those tools; and both require a bed at the pressure-melting point, since ice frozen to its bed flows by internal creep without removing material. Erosion therefore follows the structures and hollows already present: a glacier occupies a pre-existing valley, deepens and widens it into a trough, and exploits faults, joint sets and weak rock belts, while plateau shoulders and interfluves above the thick trunk ice are little altered. Hanging valleys exist precisely because tributary ice was thinner and eroded less. The qualification is that exaggeration is not the whole story: because a glacier can erode below its own outlet level, it creates closed rock basins and ribbon lakes that no previous fluvial landscape contained, and cold-based ice preserves rather than exaggerates.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 2 for plucking stated with its jointing and sliding conditions; 3 for abrasion stated with its tool, pressure and renewal conditions; 3 for selectivity derived from those conditions and illustrated at two scales; 1 for over-deepening conceded as genuinely new relief; 1 for the cold-based preservation case. Deduct 2 for presenting erosion as uniform beneath ice.

## Lesson 6 — Erosional landforms I: the upland suite

Progress: 6 / 19  |  Stage: Core  |  Subtopic: Cirque and tarn, arête, col, horn, nunatak and crag-and-tail — one genetic family drawn once on a single massif, with the excavation mechanics that generate all of them

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
