⚠️ That description is a glaciofluvial and lacustrine one in all but name. **Clays, sands and silts with conglomerate lenses from old deltaic fans** is exactly the sediment architecture of material delivered by meltwater-fed streams into a standing water body and built out as deltas — the distal end of this lesson's gradient, deposited into a lake rather than onto a plain. ⚠️ The Karewas are therefore the **best Indian representative of the glaciofluvial and proglacial-lacustrine environment** available in this lane, and Lesson 10 develops their chronological significance.

🇮🇳 ✅ A second, smaller hook: Khullar records at **Sonapani** in the Chandra valley a **dry lake basin about 2.5 kilometres by 1.5 kilometres just below the ice cliff** — a proglacial basin, now empty, of exactly the kind in which varved sediment accumulates.

❗ **The limitation, stated with the hook, and it is substantial.** ⚠️ **India has no sandur of the Icelandic kind and no classic esker or kame fields**, because those landforms require an **ice-sheet margin standing on a lowland** with vast, sustained meltwater discharge, and India had a mountain glaciation rather than an ice sheet. ⚠️ Indian glaciofluvial deposition is confined to **valley fills, outwash fans at glacier snouts, terraces along glacier-fed headstreams and lacustrine basins such as the Karewas**. ❗ Say so. ⚠️ A candidate who claims Indian eskers or sandar is making a claim that the terrain itself contradicts, and the honest alternative — naming the Karewas and the valley-fill terraces and explaining why the lowland suite is absent — is stronger.

🔁 ❗ One boundary must be visible here. **River systems, drainage patterns, basin transfer and the fluvial landform suite belong to the running-water owner.** This topic owns glaciofluvial deposition because its sediment supply, discharge regime and position are glacial; it does not own the fluvial processes that act on that sediment once it has left the glacier's influence.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| Pair | The discriminator | The wrong answer it prevents |
|---|---|---|
| **Esker / drumlin** | **Sorted gravel laid by water in a tunnel** against **unsorted till moulded by ice** | Treating two flow-parallel ridges as one landform type |
| **Esker / terminal moraine** | **Parallel to flow, gravel** against **transverse to flow, till** | Reading a ridge's orientation without reading its sediment |
| **Kame terrace / lateral moraine** | **Sorted and bedded** against **unsorted till**, in nearly the same position | Mis-reading former ice thickness from a water-laid bench |
| **Kettle hole / cirque tarn** | A **collapse hollow in sediment** against an **excavated rock basin** | Treating every small glacial lake as erosional |
| **Outwash / till** | **Sorted and stratified** against **unsorted and unstratified** | The Core owner's named trap |
| **Varve / ordinary lake bed** | A **seasonal couplet structure** against undifferentiated mud | Over-claiming an annual chronology from any laminated sediment |

**Strongest objection.** This is sedimentology for a northern European landscape. India has none of these landforms at scale, the owned demands are about fjords and water security, and the lesson could be reduced to one line in the depositional lesson.

**Reply.** Three things here are not substitutable. ❗ **First**, the **sorting rule** is the diagnostic that makes the whole depositional half of the topic testable in one line — *ice cannot sort, water must* — and it cannot be installed properly without showing what water-laid glacial sediment actually looks like. ❗ **Second**, the **buried-ice mechanism** of the kettle hole is the same mechanism that makes a moraine dam unreliable at Lesson 17; teaching it here, in a harmless landform, is what makes the hazardous version intelligible. ❗ **Third**, the **varve** is the only annual, non-instrumental chronometer in the topic, and the Karewas give India a real lacustrine record of precisely this kind — which Lesson 10 then uses. ⚠️ The objection is nonetheless half right about scale, and this lesson concedes it explicitly rather than inflating an Indian parallel.

⚠️ **Residual.** Whether a given laminated couplet set is truly annual cannot be established from the sediment alone; multiple seasonal inflow events can mimic annual banding. ❗ No varve-based age is asserted anywhere in this file.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Salient features of world's physical geography" (Mains General Studies Paper I, direct ownership).
- **Verified linkage:** no owned demand. This lesson completes the depositional half of the *distinguish erosional and depositional landforms* directive recorded by the Core owner, and it supplies the **buried-ice premise** that Lesson 17's dam-failure argument requires.
- **Probable framing:** as the sorted half of a depositional-landform demand; as the **sediment-evidence paragraph** in a palaeoclimate or chronology answer; or as the explanation of braided, sediment-choked channels below a glacier snout.
- **Traps:** calling an esker a moraine; calling a kame terrace a lateral moraine; treating a kettle lake as erosional; claiming Indian sandar or eskers; presenting varve counts as absolute dates; developing braided-channel theory, which belongs to the running-water owner.
- **Answer use:** the reusable sentence is *"Meltwater sorts what ice could not, so the glaciofluvial suite is bedded and graded — the esker is the gravel cast of a subglacial tunnel, the kame the collapsed fill of a hollow in stagnant ice, and the outwash plain a braided, aggrading spread that fines outward from the margin."*

Revision notes:

- ❗ **The rule:** ice as carrier → unsorted till and moraine; **water as carrier → sorted, stratified esker, kame, outwash and varve**.
- ✅ **Esker** = sinuous **coarse-gravel ridge** from a **subglacial meltwater stream**, **up to hundreds of km long and 100 m high**, **parallel to ice flow**.
- ❗ An esker is **the cast of a river, not the valley of one** — it can run over divides because the driving gradient was the **ice surface**.
- ✅ **Kame** = irregular mound of **stratified glaciofluvial sediment** formed during **ice stagnation** (a **Scottish** term). ⚠️ **Kame terrace** = a bench laid between the ice margin and the valley wall.
- ❗ **Kame terrace against lateral moraine:** same position, **opposite sediment** — sorted and bedded against unsorted till.
- ⚠️ **Kettle hole** = collapse hollow where **buried ice melted out** of sediment; often water-filled. ❗ The same buried-ice mechanism weakens a moraine dam at Lesson 17.
- ✅ **Outwash plain** = glacial plain **at or beyond the ice margin**, often **hundreds of metres thick**, built by **aggrading braided meltwater channels**; ✅ **sandur** = an extensive plain of glaciofluvial sands and gravels in front of an ice margin.
- ⚠️ Cause of braiding here: **sediment overload plus strongly fluctuating seasonal and diurnal discharge**. 🔁 Braiding theory itself belongs to the running-water owner.
- ⚠️ **Sorting gradient:** coarse, poorly sorted, steeply bedded **proximal** → fine, well sorted, flat-bedded **distal**.
- ⚠️ **Varve** = one summer light band plus one winter dark band = **one year**; a non-instrumental chronometer, with couplet thickness carrying a melt signal.
- ❗ Varves give **duration and sequence**, not absolute calendar age; multiple seasonal inflows can mimic annual banding.
- 🇮🇳 ✅ **Karewas** = *"flat-topped terraces of the Kashmir valley and on the flanks of the Pir Panjal consisting of clays, sands, silts together with lenticles of conglomerate from old deltaic fans"* — India's glaciofluvial and proglacial-lacustrine record.
- 🇮🇳 ✅ **Sonapani's dry proglacial basin**, about 2.5 km by 1.5 km below the ice cliff.
- ❗ **India has no sandur, esker or kame fields** — those need an **ice-sheet margin on a lowland**. Indian glaciofluvial deposition is **valley fills, snout fans, terraces and lacustrine basins**. Name the absence.

### Concept check

**Question:** A survey of a formerly glaciated lowland reports: a 40-kilometre sinuous gravel ridge running across the grain of the country and over two low watersheds; a group of irregular, steeply bedded sand-and-gravel mounds; a flat plain of bedded sands fining outward, pitted by water-filled hollows; and, in an adjacent basin, several metres of rhythmically laminated silt and clay. Identify each and state what the assemblage establishes about the deglaciation of this area.

**Model answer:** The **sinuous gravel ridge** is an **esker**: a subglacial meltwater stream deposited gravel on the floor of its ice tunnel, and when the surrounding ice melted the tunnel fill was left standing in relief. Its indifference to the local watersheds confirms the mechanism, because the stream's driving gradient was the **ice surface**, not the ground, so it could run across divides. The **irregular, steeply bedded mounds** are **kames**, sediment accumulated in hollows and crevasses within or against stagnating ice and collapsed into mounds when the support melted; their bedding shows water laid them, and their disordered form shows the ice was decaying in place rather than retreating as an active margin. The **flat plain of bedded sands fining outward** is an **outwash plain or sandur**, built by braided, aggrading meltwater channels beyond the margin, and the **water-filled hollows** pitting it are **kettle holes** where ice blocks buried in the outwash melted out afterwards. The **rhythmically laminated silt and clay** are **varves**, deposited in a proglacial lake with a coarse summer band and a fine winter band in each couplet. Together the assemblage establishes a deglaciation in which the margin **stagnated and decayed in place**, with abundant meltwater running subglacially and beyond the margin, ice blocks surviving buried in the outwash, and a proglacial lake present long enough to accumulate an annually banded record. It does not establish a calendar date, because varve counts give duration and sequence rather than absolute age.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat the kettle holes as evidence of glacial erosion. They are collapse features in a depositional body, formed after deposition by the melting out of buried ice — the same process that undermines a moraine dam, and the reason a sediment body's geometry can change long after the agent that built it has gone.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** Distinguish glacial from glaciofluvial deposition and explain why the distinction is useful in reading a landscape. *(Distinguish and explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** Glacial deposition is by ice acting directly; glaciofluvial deposition is by the meltwater the ice releases. Because ice is a solid carrying boulders and clay together at every level, material it drops is till: unsorted, unstratified, formed into moraines of several kinds and into moulded drumlins. Because water differentiates its load by size and density, material it lays is bedded and graded, forming eskers in subglacial tunnels, kames and kame terraces against stagnating ice, outwash plains that fine outward from the margin, and varves in proglacial lakes. The distinction is useful because sorting identifies the agent, and the agent identifies the setting. A bench of unsorted till on a valley side records a former ice margin and therefore ice thickness, while a bedded bench in the same position records a meltwater channel between ice and wall. The qualification is that moraines built into standing water may be partly stratified.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 2 for the agent-based definition of each class rather than a landform list; 3 for at least three glaciofluvial landforms with their settings; 2 for the sorting criterion stated as the diagnostic; 2 for the kame-terrace against lateral-moraine case used to show why the diagnostic matters; 1 for the partly-stratified qualification. Deduct 2 for calling outwash unsorted.

## Lesson 10 — Glacial cycles and the Pleistocene record

Progress: 10 / 19  |  Stage: Core  |  Subtopic: Glacials and interglacials, the classical Indian four-glacial scheme with its authors named, eustatic lowering and isostatic response, the Karewas and the Narmada alluvium, and the field evidence that ice once reached far lower than it does now

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Canonical owner span: Core owner §9.3 evidence unit 1 (L220-L225) — landform evidence allows past climates to be reconstructed where no instrument record exists, with its stated limitation that landforms record *that* ice was present rather than when; Advanced owner §1 (L18) on ancient moraines below present snouts proving that Pleistocene ice reached far lower.
Book span: D.R. Khullar, local-scan PDF p.41 (folios 48-49), *"Ice Ages in India"* — the Quaternary span, the de Terra and Paterson scheme, the eustatic lowering, the Karewas, the Narmada alluvium, the Pir Panjal uplift and the Kashmir terraces; G.C. Leong, Chapter 6, local-scan PDF p.50 — ice ages and the extent of former ice.
Current-affairs anchor: no genuine official anchor applies to this lesson. Pleistocene chronology is not the subject of any of the seven verified official documents in this lane; attaching a 2025 press release to a Quaternary scheme would be a false currency claim.
Qdrant: unavailable (non-blocking).
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

```text
   THE CLASSICAL INDIAN QUATERNARY - A TIMELINE STRIP
   ==============================================================================
   <------------------- QUATERNARY, about ONE MILLION YEARS ------------------->
   <---------------- P L E I S T O C E N E ----------------><-- RECENT -------->
                                                            (began about 12,000
                                                             years ago, with the
                                                             withdrawal of the
                                                             last glaciation)
   ..............................................................................
   de TERRA and PATERSON: FOUR glacials, THREE inter-glacials
   (a CLASSICAL scheme for India, named here with its authors, NOT presented as
    settled modern chronology)

   LOWER PLEISTOCENE  |     MIDDLE PLEISTOCENE     |    UPPER PLEISTOCENE
   [G1]===[IG1]=======|=====[G2]===[IG2]===========|====[G3]==[IG3]==[G4]=======
    ^      ^                 ^      ^                    ^           ^
    |      |                 |      |                    |           |
    |      |                 |      |                    |           +-- KASHMIR
    |      |                 |      |                    |               TERRACE
    |      |                 |      |                    |               DEPOSITS
    |      |                 |      |                    |               at its END
    |      |                 |      |                    +-- PIR PANJAL UPLIFT
    |      |                 |      |                        (also during G1)
    |      |                 |      +-- K A R E W A S of Kashmir
    |      |                 |          "supposed to be of the second
    |      |                 |           inter-glacial period"
    |      |                 |          + NARMADA alluvial deposits
    |      |                 +-- heavy BOULDER-FAN and FLUVIO-GLACIAL deposition
    |      +-- First Inter-glacial, in KASHMIR
    +-- First Glacial = LOWER PLEISTOCENE in Kashmir
   ..............................................................................
   WHAT HAPPENED WORLD-WIDE DURING EACH GLACIAL
     SEA LEVEL ~100 m LOWER   -> land area greatly EXTENDED, shelves exposed
     HIMALAYAN GLACIERS FAR LARGER
     ICE LOADS DEPRESS the crust; on melting the crust REBOUNDS (isostasy)
   ..............................................................................
   THE FIELD PROOF, AND IT IS INDIAN
     ANCIENT MORAINES LIE BELOW PRESENT SNOUTS
     -> the ice once reached FAR LOWER than it does today
     -> a landform statement that is also a CLIMATE statement
   ==============================================================================
```

One strip, one million years, four named Indian deposits. ❗ The strip is deliberately drawn with its authors' names on it, because the single most important instruction about this scheme is that it is a **classical framework attributed to named workers**, not a modern chronology — and a candidate who presents it as the latter is making a claim the source itself does not make.

### 1. The intuition before the terminology

⚠️ The landforms of Lessons 6 to 9 occur in places where there is no ice today — across northern Europe, across North America, and far below the present snouts of Himalayan glaciers. ❗ **That is the whole argument of this lesson in one observation.** Landforms are durable; ice is not. Where the landform suite is present and the ice is absent, the ice has gone.

⚠️ The Core owner states it as a formal evidence unit: *"U-shaped valleys, cirques, arêtes, horns and erratics occur far outside today's glaciated areas — including across northern Europe and North America… it proves that ice extent has changed radically, independent of any modern measurement."* ❗ And it states the limitation in the same breath: *"landforms record **that** ice was present, not precisely when or for how long, without separate dating evidence."* ⚠️ That pairing — a strong inference with an explicit limit — is the model for the entire lesson.

### 2. The span, and what the terms mean

✅ Khullar, at local-scan PDF p.41 (folio 49), supplies the frame: the **Pleistocene and Recent together — the Quaternary — span approximately one million years**, and the **Recent began about 12,000 years ago with the withdrawal of the last glaciation.**

⚠️ The vocabulary, separated once:

- ⚠️ An **ice age** or **glacial** is a prolonged period of colder global climate during which ice sheets and mountain glaciers expand greatly.
- ⚠️ An **interglacial** is a warm interval between glacials, during which ice retreats towards or beyond its present extent.
- ⚠️ The **Pleistocene** is the epoch within which this alternation occurred; the **Recent** or Holocene is the present interval since the last withdrawal.

❗ A precision point worth one clause: ⚠️ **we are in an interglacial, not after the ice age.** The alternation is the normal state of the Quaternary, and the present warm interval is one of its terms. ⚠️ That framing is useful in an answer because it separates the long-period orbital alternation from the short-period change that Lessons 14 and 15 address — and a candidate who conflates them will produce the confused argument that present glacier retreat is "just the end of the ice age".

### 3. The classical Indian scheme, with its authors and its hedge

✅ Khullar records that **de Terra and Paterson** divide the Pleistocene into **four glacial and three inter-glacial periods**, and assigns Indian deposits to them:

| ✅ Division | ✅ What Khullar records |
|---|---|
| **First Glacial and First Inter-glacial** | Correspond to the **Lower Pleistocene in Kashmir** |
| **Second Glacial and Second Inter-glacial** | **Middle Pleistocene**, with **heavy boulder-fan and fluvio-glacial deposition** |
| **The Karewas of Kashmir** | **"supposed to be of the second inter-glacial period"** — *"flat-topped terraces of the Kashmir valley and on the flanks of the Pir Panjal consisting of clays, sands, silts together with lenticles of conglomerate from old deltaic fans"* |
| **Narmada alluvial deposits** | Of the **same period** |
| **Upper Pleistocene** | Includes the **Third and Fourth glaciations and the Third Inter-Glacial** |
| **Tectonic coupling** | **Pir Panjal uplift during the First and Third Glacials**; **Kashmir terrace deposits at the end of the Fourth Glaciation** |

❗ **Three handling instructions, all binding.**

1. ❗ **Name the authors.** The scheme is *de Terra and Paterson's*, and attributing it is both honest and examination-safe.
2. ❗ **Preserve the hedge.** The source says the Karewas are **"supposed to be"** of the second interglacial. ⚠️ Reproduce that hedge; do not convert a qualified attribution into a flat assertion.
3. ❗ **Attach no absolute dates.** ⚠️ The source gives none for the individual glacials, and supplying them from memory would be exactly the invented-number failure this topic's governing caution forbids. ⚠️ Write "the classical Indian scheme assigns the Karewas to the second interglacial", not "the Karewas date to X years ago".

⚠️ The honest status statement, worth carrying verbatim into an answer: *"De Terra and Paterson's four-glacial and three-interglacial division is the classical framework for the Indian Pleistocene and remains the standard teaching scheme in the Indian geographical literature; modern Quaternary chronology is built on continuous marine and ice-core records rather than on a small number of named glacials, so the scheme should be cited with its authors as a regional framework rather than as a settled global chronology."*

### 4. Eustasy and isostasy — the two sea-level consequences

✅ Khullar records that during glacials **sea level stood about 100 metres lower**, greatly extending the land area, and that Himalayan glaciers were far larger.

⚠️ Two distinct mechanisms are involved and conflating them is a common error.

| ⚠️ Mechanism | What changes | Direction during a glacial | Direction after deglaciation |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Eustatic** | The **volume of water in the ocean**, because water is locked up as land ice | Sea level **falls** globally | Sea level **rises** globally |
| **Isostatic** | The **height of the land**, because an ice load depresses the crust into the mantle | Land **subsides** under the ice | Land **rebounds** after the ice melts, over thousands of years |

❗ Three examinable consequences follow.

1. ⚠️ **The exposure of continental shelves** during glacials extended land area and created land bridges, with consequences for biogeography and for human dispersal. 🔁 Those consequences belong to other owners and are named, not developed.
2. ⚠️ **Post-glacial eustatic rise is what drowned the glacial troughs** of Lesson 7 to produce fjords. ❗ The fjord derivation therefore depends on this lesson, and a strong 2023-style answer says so.
3. ⚠️ **Relative sea level at any coast is the net of the two** — global water volume against local land height. 🔁 Relative sea level and its coastal consequences belong to the coastal-landforms owner; this topic supplies the cryospheric cause and stops.

### 5. The periglacial margin, and why it matters in India

⚠️ Beyond the ice limit lies the **periglacial** zone: ground that is not glaciated but is dominated by intense freeze-thaw, frozen ground and slope processes. ⚠️ During glacials this zone extended far beyond the ice margin, and its characteristic processes — frost shattering, solifluction, patterned ground — acted on terrain that is temperate today.

🔁 ❗ **Frost weathering, solifluction and slope-failure mechanics belong to the weathering, mass-movement and groundwater owner.** ⚠️ This topic names the periglacial margin for two reasons only: it explains the **boulder-fan and fluvio-glacial deposition** that Khullar records for the Middle Pleistocene, and it is the source of the **debris supply** that Lesson 2's debris-cover effect and Lesson 8's lateral moraines both require.

### 6. 🇮🇳 India hook — moraines below snouts, and the limitation on dating them

🇮🇳 ✅ The decisive Indian field evidence is the Advanced owner's: **ancient moraines lie below present snouts**, proving that Pleistocene ice reached far lower than it does now. ⚠️ This is the Indian form of the Core owner's global evidence unit, and it is better than the global form for an Indian paper because it is local, visible and checkable.

🇮🇳 ⚠️ The Karewas add a second, different kind of evidence. ✅ Their composition — **clays, sands, silts with conglomerate lenses from old deltaic fans** — is the signature of sediment delivered into a standing water body, which means the Kashmir valley held a substantial lake during their deposition. ⚠️ A lake of that kind in an intermontane basin flanked by a rising range is consistent with a glacial and fluvio-glacial sediment supply and with drainage impounded by tectonic uplift — and ✅ Khullar couples the two explicitly by recording **Pir Panjal uplift during the First and Third Glacials**.

❗ **The limitation, stated with the hook, and it is the central one of this lesson.** ⚠️ Moraines below present snouts establish **that ice was lower**, not **when it was lower or by how much over what interval**. ⚠️ The Karewas are assigned to the second interglacial by a **named classical scheme with the source's own hedge attached**, and no absolute age is available in this lane. ❗ A candidate should therefore write: *"ancient moraines below present snouts show that Himalayan ice formerly extended much further down-valley; their assignment within the Pleistocene follows de Terra and Paterson's classical scheme, and no absolute age is asserted here."* ⚠️ That sentence is both the strongest available claim and an honest one, and the honesty is itself creditable because this topic's governing instruction forbids inventing chronology.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| Pair | The discriminator | The wrong answer it prevents |
|---|---|---|
| **Glacial / interglacial** | A cold expansion phase against a warm contraction phase within the same epoch | Treating "the ice age" as a single past event |
| **Pleistocene / Recent** | The epoch of alternation against the interval since the last withdrawal, about 12,000 years | Saying the ice age "ended" rather than that the present is an interglacial |
| **Eustatic / isostatic** | **Water volume** against **land height** | Explaining a raised beach by ocean volume alone, or a drowned valley by subsidence alone |
| **Moraine below a snout / recessional moraine** | Evidence of a **far larger former glacier** against evidence of a **stillstand in the present retreat** | Over- or under-stating the magnitude of past change |
| **Varve count / absolute date** | **Duration and sequence** against **calendar age** | Presenting a counted sequence as a dated one |

**Strongest objection.** The de Terra and Paterson scheme is close to a century old and has been superseded by continuous marine-isotope and ice-core records. Teaching it to a Mains candidate risks installing an obsolete chronology.

**Reply.** The objection is correct about the science and wrong about the remedy. ⚠️ The scheme is **the framework in which the Indian deposits are described in the Indian literature**, including the Karewa and Narmada assignments, so a candidate who does not hold it cannot read the Indian material at all. ❗ The correct response is therefore not to omit the scheme but to **cite it as a named regional framework with its authors attached, preserve the source's own hedge, and refuse to supply absolute dates** — which is exactly what this lesson does, and which is itself an examinable demonstration of source discipline. ⚠️ A candidate who writes "the classical Indian scheme of de Terra and Paterson assigns these to the second interglacial, though modern Quaternary chronology rests on continuous records rather than a fourfold division" has shown command of both the material and its status.

⚠️ **Residual.** The correlation between the Indian glacial sequence and global Quaternary chronology is **not established by any source available to this edition**, and the number of Himalayan glacial advances is not something this evidence base can settle. ❗ No correlation is asserted.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Salient features of world's physical geography" (Mains General Studies Paper I, direct ownership).
- **Verified linkage:** no owned demand. ⚠️ This lesson supplies the **eustatic premise of the fjord derivation** that the owned 2023 demand requires, and it supplies the **palaeo-baseline** against which Lesson 14's modern measurement discussion is set.
- **Probable framing:** as a **background paragraph** establishing that ice extent has changed radically, as the **Indian deposit set** in a question on the Quaternary, or as the **evidence clause** in any answer that asserts past climatic change.
- **Traps:** presenting the fourfold scheme as settled modern chronology; dropping the source's "supposed to be" hedge; attaching absolute dates; conflating eustasy and isostasy; saying the ice age ended rather than that the present is an interglacial; treating moraines below snouts as dating evidence.
- **Answer use:** the reusable pair is *"Glacial landforms occur far beyond today's ice, including ancient moraines below present Himalayan snouts, which establishes that ice extent has changed radically independent of any instrument record"* followed by *"— although landforms record that ice was present rather than precisely when, so chronology requires separate dating evidence."*

Revision notes:

- ✅ **Quaternary = Pleistocene + Recent ≈ one million years**; ✅ the **Recent began about 12,000 years ago** with the withdrawal of the last glaciation.
- ⚠️ **Glacial** = cold expansion phase; **interglacial** = warm contraction phase. ❗ **We are in an interglacial**, not after the ice age.
- ✅ **de Terra and Paterson: four glacials, three inter-glacials.** ❗ Name the authors; ❗ treat it as the **classical Indian framework**, not settled modern chronology; ❗ attach **no absolute dates**.
- ✅ **First Glacial / Inter-glacial = Lower Pleistocene in Kashmir**; **Second = Middle Pleistocene** with **heavy boulder-fan and fluvio-glacial deposition**; **Upper Pleistocene = Third and Fourth glaciations plus the Third Inter-Glacial**.
- ✅ **Karewas** = *"flat-topped terraces of the Kashmir valley and on the flanks of the Pir Panjal consisting of clays, sands, silts together with lenticles of conglomerate from old deltaic fans"*, **"supposed to be of the second inter-glacial period"**. ❗ Keep the hedge.
- ✅ **Narmada alluvial deposits** of the same period.
- ✅ **Pir Panjal uplift during the First and Third Glacials**; ✅ **Kashmir terrace deposits at the end of the Fourth Glaciation**.
- ✅ During glacials **sea level stood about 100 m lower** and Himalayan glaciers were far larger.
- ❗ **Eustatic = water volume; isostatic = land height.** Glacial: eustatic fall plus isostatic depression. Post-glacial: eustatic rise plus slow isostatic rebound.
- ⚠️ **Post-glacial eustatic rise drowned the troughs that became fjords** (Lesson 7). 🔁 Relative sea level and coasts → coastal-landforms owner.
- ⚠️ **Periglacial margin** beyond the ice limit — frost shattering, frozen ground, solifluction. 🔁 Those processes belong to the weathering and mass-movement owner; used here only as a **debris supply** and as the setting of the boulder-fan deposition.
- 🇮🇳 ✅ **Ancient moraines below present snouts** prove ice once reached far lower — the best Indian evidence in the topic.
- ❗ Landforms record **that** ice was present, **not when**; no absolute age, no global correlation and no count of Himalayan advances is asserted in this file.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "India's last ice age ended 12,000 years ago, when the four glacial periods identified by de Terra and Paterson finally finished and sea level rose by 100 metres, drowning the valleys to make fjords." Identify what is accurate, what is mis-stated and what is unsupported, and give a corrected version.

**Model answer:** One element is accurate, three are mis-stated and one is unsupported. **Accurate:** the Recent did begin about 12,000 years ago with the withdrawal of the last glaciation, and sea level during glacials did stand about 100 metres lower, so post-glacial eustatic rise is of that order. **Mis-stated, first:** "the ice age ended" misdescribes the structure of the Quaternary. The Pleistocene is an epoch of alternation between glacials and interglacials, and the present is an **interglacial** within it, not a period after it. **Mis-stated, second:** the fourfold division is **de Terra and Paterson's classical framework for the Indian Pleistocene**, and should be cited with its authors as a regional scheme rather than presented as a settled chronology that "finished"; modern Quaternary chronology rests on continuous records rather than a small number of named glacials. **Mis-stated, third:** India has **no fjords**. Post-glacial drowning produced fjords on formerly glaciated mountainous coasts in mid and high latitudes; India's ice lay far inland at high altitude, so no Indian trough was over-deepened below sea level. **Unsupported:** no absolute date is available in this evidence base for any individual glacial or for the Karewas, whose assignment to the second interglacial the source itself hedges as "supposed to be". A corrected version: *"The Indian Pleistocene is classically divided by de Terra and Paterson into four glacials and three interglacials, with the Karewas of Kashmir assigned to the second interglacial; the Recent began about 12,000 years ago, and the eustatic rise of roughly 100 metres that followed deglaciation drowned glacial troughs on mountainous mid- and high-latitude coasts to form fjords — none of them in India."*

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not convert a named classical scheme into a dated chronology by supplying years. The source gives a sequence and a regional correlation, not ages, and a fabricated date is identifiable with certainty by anyone who knows the literature.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "Glacial landforms are a climate record that needs no instruments." Examine, with Indian evidence. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The claim is strong but bounded. Landforms are durable while ice is not, so a glacial suite in an unglaciated place records vanished ice directly. Cirques, arêtes, horns, troughs and erratics occur far beyond today's ice in northern Europe and North America, and in India ancient moraines lie below present snouts, establishing that Himalayan ice formerly reached much further down-valley. Lateral moraines perched above a modern glacier record its former thickness, and the angular-above, smoothed-below contrast records the former ice surface. The Karewas of Kashmir — flat-topped terraces of clays, sands and silts with conglomerate lenses from old deltaic fans — record a standing water body fed by fluvio-glacial sediment. The bound is that landforms record that ice was present, not when or for how long. Chronology requires separate dating evidence, which is why the Indian sequence is cited through de Terra and Paterson's classical scheme rather than in years.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for the durability argument stated as the reason landforms are a record; 3 for at least three distinct Indian or named evidence types rather than one; 2 for the limitation that landforms date nothing by themselves; 2 for the classical scheme cited with its authors and no absolute age supplied. Deduct 2 for asserting a date not carried by any source and 2 for claiming Indian fjords.

| ✅ State or Union Territory | ✅ Glaciers | ✅ Area (km²) |
|---|---:|---:|
| Jammu and Kashmir, and Ladakh | **5,262** | **29,163** |
| Himachal Pradesh | **2,735** | **4,516** |
| Uttarakhand | **968** | **2,857** |
| Sikkim | **449** | **706** |
| Arunachal Pradesh | **161** | **223** |
| **Total** | **9,575** | **37,465** |

✅ **Source:** Geological Survey of India, *Inventory of Himalayan Glaciers*, **Special Publication No. 34, 2009**, as cited by the Ministry of Jal Shakti in its answer to **Lok Sabha Unstarred Question No. 3249, 20 March 2025**, Annexure-I. ❗ **Quote it with all three elements — the inventory, its year and the answering ministry — every time.**

### 1. The Arc-A mechanism, applied

🔁 ⚠️ **This is the existence condition from Lesson 1 and the equilibrium-line logic from Lesson 2, applied to a mountain range.** A glacier requires a surplus of solid precipitation over loss, sustained over years, and enough thickness to flow. In a mountain range that reduces to a single question: **is there enough area standing above the equilibrium line, and does enough snow fall on it?** ❗ Everything in this lesson is the Himalayan answer to that question, and the answer has two halves — an altitude half and a moisture half — which pull in different directions across the range.

### 2. The sector framework, and the rule it generates

⚠️ Read the transect from south to north. The **Shiwalik** and the **Lesser or Middle Himalaya** are comparatively low; the **Greater or Inner Himalaya** carries the highest summits; beyond it the **Ladakh** and **Zanskar** ranges and the **Karakoram** stand high but in the rain shadow of everything to the south.

✅ The Advanced owner states the resulting distribution directly: **most large Indian glaciers lie in the Greater Himalaya and the Trans-Himalaya — the Karakoram, Ladakh and Zanskar — while the Lesser Himalaya has only small glaciers, with traces in the Pir Panjal and the Dhauladhar.**

❗ **The trap, named explicitly by the Advanced owner:** ❌ *"Most Indian glaciers are in the Lesser Himalaya."* ⚠️ They are not. ⚠️ The misconception is natural, because the Lesser Himalaya is the part of the range most people see and the part most hill stations occupy — but it is **too low to hold a substantial accumulation zone above the equilibrium line**.

⚠️ The two-variable explanation is the mark-earning version, and it is a direct application of Lesson 2's ledger:

| Sector | Altitude | Moisture supply | ⚠️ Resulting ice |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Shiwalik** | Low | Monsoon-rich, but as rain | None |
| **Lesser or Middle Himalaya** | Moderate | Monsoon-rich | **Small and scattered only**; traces in the Pir Panjal and Dhauladhar |
| **Greater or Inner Himalaya** | Very high | Substantial, monsoon in the east and centre, westerly in the west | **Major valley glaciers** |
| **Zanskar and Ladakh** | Very high | Drier, in the rain shadow | **Major glaciers, higher equilibrium line** |
| **Karakoram** | Highest and most extensive high ground | ✅ **"High, arid snowline"** (Advanced owner); total precipitation low but ✅ Khullar notes that in the western Himalaya *"it all falls as snow"* | **The largest concentration in the whole region** |

❗ The Karakoram row is the one that teaches the mechanism rather than the fact. ⚠️ It receives less total precipitation than the eastern Himalaya, yet it carries the greatest development of glaciers — because what matters to the ledger is **the fraction of precipitation that falls and remains as snow**, not the total depth of precipitation. ⚠️ A cold, high range converts almost all of its modest precipitation into accumulation; a warm, wet, lower range converts most of its much larger precipitation into rain and runoff.

### 3. The syntaxial-bend framing, and why it is a boundary statement

✅ Both Indian textbooks frame the Himalayan glacier estate as lying **"between the two syntaxial bends"**. ⚠️ A syntaxial bend is the sharp hairpin at which the Himalayan arc turns: the **western** bend around the Hindukush, Karakoram and Pamir, and the **eastern** bend where the Eastern Himalaya meets the Patkai ranges in Arunachal Pradesh.

❗ The framing is geomorphologically useful and politically treacherous in equal measure. ⚠️ It defines the **mountain arc** as a single physiographic unit, which is correct — but that arc **spans Pakistan-administered territory, Nepal, Bhutan, Tibet and India**. ❗ **A count over the arc is therefore not a count over India**, and that single sentence is the whole of the correction that follows.

### 4. ❌ CORRECTION BOX — the glacier count

```text
   +==========================================================================+
   |  CORRECTION: "ABOUT 15,000 GLACIERS IN THE HIMALAYA"                     |
   +==========================================================================+
   |  WHAT THE BOOKS SAY                                                      |
   |    Khullar, local-scan PDF p.50 (folio 67):                              |
   |      "There are about 15,000 glaciers in the Himalayas lying between     |
   |       the two syntaxial bends in the east and west."                     |
   |    Majid Husain, printed p.10.16 (local scan PDF p.353):                 |
   |      "There are nearly 15,000 glaciers in Himalayas lying between the    |
   |       two syntaxial [bend]s..."                                          |
   |    Both canonical owners already flag this as a SOURCE-ERA estimate.     |
   |                                                                          |
   |  WHAT THE VERIFIED OFFICIAL RECORD SAYS                                  |
   |    Geological Survey of India, Inventory of Himalayan Glaciers,          |
   |    Special Publication No. 34, 2009, cited by the Ministry of Jal        |
   |    Shakti in Lok Sabha Unstarred Question No. 3249, 20 March 2025:       |
   |      "the country has a total of 9,575 glaciers" - 37,465 sq km,         |
   |      with the state-wise distribution tabulated above.                   |
   |                                                                          |
   |  THE RESOLUTION - AND IT IS NOT WHAT MOST CANDIDATES ASSUME              |
   |    THE TWO FIGURES ARE NOT IN CONFLICT.                                  |
   |    ~15,000 counts glaciers over the WHOLE HIMALAYAN ARC between the      |
   |      syntaxial bends - Pakistan-administered territory, Nepal, Bhutan    |
   |      and Tibet INCLUDED.                                                 |
   |    9,575 counts glaciers WITHIN INDIA under a stated national            |
   |      inventory of a stated year.                                         |
   |                                                                          |
   |  THEREFORE, AND THESE ARE PROHIBITIONS:                                  |
   |    NEVER present ~15,000 as "the number of Indian glaciers".             |
   |    NEVER present 9,575 as a DECLINE from 15,000. It is not a trend.      |
   |      It is a different count, of a different area, in a different year,  |
   |      by a different method.                                              |
   |                                                                          |
   |  THE GENERALISATION WORTH CARRYING INTO EVERY ANSWER                     |
   |    A GLACIER COUNT IS A FUNCTION OF (1) the inventory BOUNDARY,          |
   |    (2) the MINIMUM SIZE THRESHOLD, (3) the IMAGERY OR SURVEY METHOD,     |
   |    and (4) the YEAR. Change any one and the count changes.               |
   +==========================================================================+
```

⚠️ The generalisation is worth more than either number. ❗ It is the date-and-data control the Core owner states — *counts, areas, retreat rates, mass balance and hazard classes require inventory boundary, sensor or method, observation period and issuing agency* — made concrete on the single most-quoted statistic in the topic.

### 5. ❌ CORRECTION BOX — snow-covered area is not glacierised area

```text
   +==========================================================================+
   |  CORRECTION: ~33,000 sq km (books) AGAINST 37,465 sq km (GSI 2009)       |
   +==========================================================================+
   |  Khullar (folio 67) and Majid Husain (printed p.10.16) both record that  |
   |  of a total Himalayan area of about FIVE LAKH sq km, about 33,000 sq km  |
   |  - some 17 per cent - is SNOW-COVERED, with the Karakoram alone at       |
   |  about 16,000 sq km, which Khullar calls about HALF the snow-bound area  |
   |  of the Himalayan region.                                                |
   |                                                                          |
   |  GSI SP-34, 2009 gives India's GLACIERISED area as 37,465 sq km.         |
   |                                                                          |
   |  THESE ARE DIFFERENT QUANTITIES.                                         |
   |    SNOW-COVERED  = area under snow, which includes SEASONAL snow and     |
   |                    varies through the year.                              |
   |    GLACIERISED   = area occupied by GLACIERS, a perennial ice body       |
   |                    count, measured to an inventory threshold.            |
   |  AND they cover DIFFERENT AREAS: the books' 33,000 sq km refers to the   |
   |  whole Himalayan arc; the GSI figure refers to India.                    |
   |                                                                          |
   |  THEREFORE: NEVER present 33,000 -> 37,465 as GROWTH. State the          |
   |  definitional difference BEFORE either figure is used.                   |
   +==========================================================================+
```

### 6. Reading the verified table analytically

⚠️ The state table is not a list to memorise; it is a distribution to interpret, and three readings earn marks.

1. ⚠️ **Concentration.** Jammu and Kashmir together with Ladakh hold **about 55 per cent of India's glaciers and about 78 per cent of its glacierised area** — which means the average glacier there is far larger than elsewhere. ❗ That is the Karakoram, Zanskar and Ladakh signature, and it corroborates the Advanced owner's statement about where the large glaciers are without needing a second source.
2. ⚠️ **The size gradient from west to east.** Himachal has 2,735 glaciers over 4,516 square kilometres — an average of well under two square kilometres each — while the western sector's average is far higher. ⚠️ Sikkim and Arunachal have few glaciers and small areas. ❗ The eastern Himalaya is wetter but lower in its glacier-bearing belt and far steeper, so its ice is distributed as many small bodies rather than as a few large ones.
3. ⚠️ **What a count is sensitive to.** ❗ Because so many Indian glaciers are small, **the total count is extremely sensitive to the minimum mapped size**, which is precisely why two inventories of the same terrain can differ by thousands. ⚠️ The area total is far more robust than the count, and a candidate who prefers area to count when making a comparison is making a methodologically better argument.

### 7. 🇮🇳 India hook — the structural frame taken as given, and the limitation

🇮🇳 ✅ The transect above is built on **Fig. 2.11, "Physiographic Divisions of the Himalayas"**, and **Fig. 2.12(a), "Himalayan Complex"**, in Majid Husain's *Geography of India* at local-scan PDF p.42 (printed 2.18), which run from the Indo-Gangetic Plain through the Himalayan Frontal Fault, the Shiwalik, the Main Boundary Thrust, the Middle or Lesser Himalaya, the Main Central Thrust, the Greater or Inner Himalaya, the Ladakh Range, the Kailash Range and on to the Tibetan Plateau.

🔁 ❗ **Why the Himalaya exists at all — the collision, the orogeny, and the thrust system named on that transect — belongs to the crust-and-rocks owner.** ⚠️ This topic takes the structural frame **as given** and uses it for one purpose only: to say **where the ice sits within it**. No orogeny is taught here.

❗ **The limitation, stated with the hook.** ⚠️ The GSI inventory is dated **2009**, and the parliamentary answer that cites it is dated **2025** — which tells you that this remains the official national inventory, not that the 2009 numbers describe 2025 conditions. ❗ Quote the inventory with its year and present it as **the national inventory of record**, never as a current measurement. ⚠️ And the figures are a **count and an area**, not a volume or a water store; no water-equivalent quantity may be derived from them, and none is asserted anywhere in this file.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| Pair | The discriminator | The wrong answer it prevents |
|---|---|---|
| **Arc-wide count / national count** | The **inventory boundary** | "India has about 15,000 glaciers" |
| **Snow-covered / glacierised** | **Seasonal snow included** against **perennial ice only** | Reading 33,000 and 37,465 as a trend |
| **Greater and Trans-Himalaya / Lesser Himalaya** | **Altitude sufficient to hold an accumulation zone** | The Advanced owner's named trap |
| **Total precipitation / snowfall** | What **falls and remains as snow** | "The Karakoram has more glaciers because it is wetter" |
| **Glacier count / glacierised area** | Count is **threshold-sensitive**; area is **robust** | Comparing two inventories by their counts |

**Strongest objection.** The verified inventory is from 2009 and the books' estimate is older still. A candidate who quotes either is quoting a dated figure, so the safest course is to quote no number at all and argue qualitatively.

**Reply.** The objection correctly identifies the risk and draws the wrong conclusion. ⚠️ A qualitative answer that says "there are many Himalayan glaciers" is weaker than one that says *"the Geological Survey of India's Inventory of Himalayan Glaciers, Special Publication No. 34 of 2009, cited by the Ministry of Jal Shakti in March 2025, records 9,575 glaciers over 37,465 square kilometres in India, with Jammu and Kashmir together with Ladakh holding the great majority of the area."* ❗ The second answer is **more** defensible than the first, not less, because it carries its own provenance and therefore cannot be refuted by a newer figure — it was never claiming to be current. ⚠️ The discipline is not to avoid numbers; it is to **attach the inventory, the boundary, the method and the year**, so that the figure's status is part of the claim.

⚠️ **Residual.** The GSI inventory's **minimum mapped size threshold and survey method are not stated in the parliamentary answer** available to this edition, so the 2009 count cannot be rigorously compared with any satellite-derived inventory. ❗ No such comparison is made anywhere in this file.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Salient features of world's physical geography" (Mains General Studies Paper I, direct ownership). ⚠️ This lesson is also the geographical base of the *ice-caps and their changes* demand family.
- **Verified linkage:** no demand is owned by this lesson alone, but it is the **locational premise of the 2019 objective demand** on Himalayan rivers and their glacier sources, which Lesson 13 handles answer-neutrally, and the **spatial frame of the 2020 ten-mark water-resources demand** that Lesson 15 carries.
- **Probable framing:** as the **first substantive paragraph** of any Himalayan glacier answer — where the ice is, how much of it there is, on whose inventory, and why it sits where it does.
- **Traps:** quoting about 15,000 as an Indian count; presenting 9,575 as a decline; comparing snow-covered area with glacierised area; placing most Indian glaciers in the Lesser Himalaya; explaining Karakoram glaciation by high precipitation totals; quoting the 2009 inventory as a current measurement; deriving a water volume from an area.
- **Answer use:** the reusable opening is *"India's glaciers are concentrated in the Greater Himalaya and the Trans-Himalaya, because only there does enough ground stand above the equilibrium line with enough precipitation falling and remaining as snow; the Geological Survey of India's 2009 inventory, cited by the Ministry of Jal Shakti in 2025, records 9,575 glaciers over 37,465 square kilometres, of which Jammu and Kashmir with Ladakh account for the great majority of the area."*

Revision notes:

- 🔁 ⚠️ This lesson is **Lesson 2's equilibrium-line ledger applied to a mountain range**: is there enough ground above the equilibrium line, and does enough snow fall on it?
- ✅ **Most large Indian glaciers lie in the Greater Himalaya and Trans-Himalaya — Karakoram, Ladakh, Zanskar**; ✅ the Lesser Himalaya has **only small glaciers**, traces in the **Pir Panjal and Dhauladhar**.
- ❗ ❌ **Trap: "most Indian glaciers are in the Lesser Himalaya."** It is **too low** to hold an accumulation zone.
- ⚠️ **Karakoram carries the greatest development despite lower total precipitation**, because almost all of it **falls and remains as snow**; ✅ the Advanced owner records a **"high, arid snowline"** there.
- ⚠️ **Syntaxial bends:** western (Hindukush-Karakoram-Pamir) and eastern (Eastern Himalaya-Patkai, Arunachal Pradesh). ❗ The arc between them spans **Pakistan-administered territory, Nepal, Bhutan and Tibet** as well as India.
- ❌ ❗ **~15,000 is an ARC-WIDE, BOOK-ERA estimate — not an Indian count.**
- ✅ **GSI, *Inventory of Himalayan Glaciers*, Special Publication No. 34, 2009**, cited by the **Ministry of Jal Shakti, Lok Sabha Unstarred Q 3249, 20 March 2025: India has 9,575 glaciers over 37,465 km².**
- ✅ State split: **J&K and Ladakh 5,262 / 29,163 · Himachal 2,735 / 4,516 · Uttarakhand 968 / 2,857 · Sikkim 449 / 706 · Arunachal 161 / 223**.
- ❗ **9,575 is NOT a decline from 15,000.** Different boundary, different year, different method.
- ❗ **A glacier count is a function of boundary, minimum size threshold, method and year.** Change one and the count changes.
- ❌ ❗ **Snow-covered ≠ glacierised.** Books: ~**33,000 km² snow-covered** of a ~**five lakh km²** Himalayan area (**17 per cent**), **Karakoram ~16,000 km²**, which Khullar calls about **half the snow-bound area**. ❗ Never read 33,000 → 37,465 as growth.
- ⚠️ **J&K with Ladakh ≈ 55 per cent of glaciers but ≈ 78 per cent of area** → far larger average glacier size in the west.
- ⚠️ **Counts are threshold-sensitive; areas are robust.** Prefer area when comparing inventories.
- 🔁 **Himalayan orogeny and the thrust system (HFF, MBT, MCT) → crust-and-rocks owner.** Structure taken as given.
- ❗ **No water volume or water-equivalent store is derived from a count or an area** anywhere in this file.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "India had about 15,000 Himalayan glaciers covering 33,000 square kilometres, but the Geological Survey of India now records only 9,575 glaciers over 37,465 square kilometres, showing that about a third of India's glaciers have disappeared." Identify every error and state what the two sets of figures can legitimately be used to show.

**Model answer:** The sentence contains three distinct errors and one impossible inference. **First, a boundary error:** the figure of about 15,000 is a book-era estimate for glaciers across the **whole Himalayan arc between the two syntaxial bends**, which spans Pakistan-administered territory, Nepal, Bhutan and Tibet as well as India. It was never a count of Indian glaciers. **Second, a definitional error:** the roughly 33,000 square kilometres in the same books is **snow-covered** area over that same arc — a quantity that includes seasonal snow and varies through the year — whereas 37,465 square kilometres is India's **glacierised** area, the area occupied by perennial ice bodies. The two quantities measure different things over different areas. **Third, a method error:** the Geological Survey of India figure comes from the *Inventory of Himalayan Glaciers*, Special Publication No. 34 of **2009**, cited by the Ministry of Jal Shakti in March 2025; it is the national inventory of record, not a current measurement, and its count depends on the minimum mapped size and survey method it used. **The impossible inference** is the arithmetic itself: subtracting a national count from an arc-wide estimate produces a number that measures nothing, and the direction of the supposed change is contradicted by the candidate's own area figures, which rise. What the two sets can legitimately show is that **a glacier count is a function of the inventory boundary, the minimum size threshold, the method and the year**, and that India's own verified inventory records 9,575 glaciers over 37,465 square kilometres, concentrated overwhelmingly in Jammu and Kashmir with Ladakh.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat two numbers about glaciers as comparable merely because both are about glaciers. Comparability requires the same boundary, the same threshold, the same method and a stated interval, and in this case none of the four is shared — which is why the correct response is to report both figures with their provenance rather than to subtract them.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** Account for the distribution of India's glaciers across the Himalayan ranges. *(Account for — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The distribution follows from the mass-balance condition applied to altitude and moisture. A glacier needs ground standing above the equilibrium line and enough precipitation falling and remaining as snow on it. The Shiwalik and the Lesser Himalaya are too low, so they carry only scattered traces in the Pir Panjal and Dhauladhar. The Greater Himalaya is high enough and still snowy, so it carries major valley glaciers. The Zanskar, Ladakh and especially Karakoram ranges lie in rain shadow and receive less precipitation, yet carry the greatest concentration, because at those altitudes almost all of it falls and remains as snow. The verified inventory confirms the pattern: the Geological Survey of India's 2009 inventory, cited by the Ministry of Jal Shakti in 2025, records 9,575 glaciers over 37,465 square kilometres, with Jammu and Kashmir and Ladakh holding about 78 per cent of the area. The qualification is that counts are threshold-sensitive.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 4 for altitude and moisture used together as the explanatory pair rather than altitude alone; 3 for the Karakoram case explained by the snow fraction rather than by precipitation total; 2 for the verified inventory quoted with agency, publication and year; 1 for the Lesser Himalaya trap denied explicitly and the threshold-sensitivity qualification made. Deduct 3 for presenting the arc-wide estimate as an Indian count.

   |   Majid Husain, printed p.10.18 (local scan PDF p.355):                |
   |     the snow line lies at a LOWER elevation on the SOUTHERN slopes     |
   |     than on the northern "because the southern slopes receive much     |
   |     greater rainfall and are also much steeper."                       |
   |                                                                        |
   |   Khullar, local-scan PDF p.50 (folio 67):                             |
   |     the snow line is at a LOWER elevation on the NORTHERN slopes       |
   |     of the Great Himalaya than on the southern -- AND GIVES THE SAME   |
   |     EXPLANATION, that the southern slopes are steeper and receive      |
