```text
   THE ANSWER SPINE AS AN ARGUMENT TREE
   ==============================================================================
   [1] THESIS
    |   The significance of Himalayan glacier change lies less in the ICE
    |   than in the SEASONAL WATER-STORAGE SERVICE it performs for the most
    |   densely populated river basins on Earth - and in the HAZARD it
    |   releases while retreating.
    |
    +--[2] MECHANISM
    |       mass balance and the equilibrium line (Lesson 2)
    |       the snout retreats WHILE THE ICE STILL FLOWS FORWARD
    |       response is LAGGED and SIZE-DEPENDENT
    |
    +--[3] COMPLICATION THAT SHOWS COMMAND
    |       debris cover, elevation and size make a single range-wide
    |       statement unsafe; THINNING and SNOUT RETREAT are different
    |       measures (Lesson 14); the official finding is HETEROGENEOUS
    |
    +--[4] EFFECTS, IN DESCENDING ORDER OF CERTAINTY
    |       (a) altered DRY-SEASON FLOW RELIABILITY
    |       (b) expanding GLACIAL LAKES and GLOF exposure  (L16, L17)
    |       (c) SLOPE INSTABILITY and SEDIMENT FLUX
    |       (d) contribution to SEA-LEVEL RISE via grounded ice (L4)
    |
    +--[5] *** THE BALANCE - THE BRANCH MOST ANSWERS OMIT ***
    |       +==================================================================+
    |       |  MONSOON RAINFALL, NOT MELT, SUPPLIES THE BULK OF ANNUAL FLOW    |
    |       |  IN MOST OF THESE BASINS.                                        |
    |       |  THEREFORE the melt contribution matters DISPROPORTIONATELY      |
    |       |  IN THE LEAN SEASON AND IN DROUGHT YEARS - not in the annual     |
    |       |  total. Saying this PREVENTS THE COMMON EXAGGERATION.            |
    |       +==================================================================+
    |
    +--[6] RESPONSE
    |       monitoring and inventory | lake risk-reduction engineering |
    |       early warning | hazard-informed siting | basin-scale storage
    |       and DEMAND MANAGEMENT      [ institutional doctrine ROUTED ]
    |
    +--[7] CONCLUSION, GRADED
            the water risk is a TIMING AND RELIABILITY risk, not an
            annual-volume risk; and the hazard risk is a JOINT PRODUCT of
            cryospheric change and valley-floor development.
   ==============================================================================
   THE PEAK-WATER CURVE, DRAWN ONCE
     runoff
       ^          ___peak___
       |        _/          \_
       |      _/               \__
       |    _/                     \____
       |  _/                             \______
       +---------------------------------------------> time
         MORE ice melt first (a retreating glacier releases stored water
         FASTER), THEN LESS as the ice that supplied it is gone.
     ==> an INCREASE in flow is NOT reassurance. It is the first half of
         a decline.
```

### 1. The Arc-A mechanism, applied

🔁 ⚠️ **This is Lesson 2's balance ledger and Lesson 4's grounded-ice rule, applied to a water-resources question.** ❗ Step two of the spine *is* Lesson 2; the fourth effect pathway *is* Lesson 4's floating-against-grounded rule; and step three is Lesson 14's measurement discipline compressed into a clause. ⚠️ The spine is therefore not a new body of content — it is an **ordering** of content already built, and that is precisely why it is reusable.

### 2. Why the thesis must be about storage, not about ice

⚠️ The instinctive opening — "Himalayan glaciers are melting, which threatens India's water supply" — concedes the argument before making it, because it invites the obvious rebuttal that melting ice **increases** flow. ❗ The Core owner's thesis avoids this by relocating the significance: the glaciers matter as a **seasonal store**, not as a volume.

⚠️ The mechanism of the store, in one paragraph. Precipitation in the high Himalaya falls largely as snow and is held as ice. Ice releases water by melting, and melting is concentrated in the warm months and in warm, dry, clear-sky conditions — ❗ which is to say, **precisely when rainfall is least and demand is greatest**. ⚠️ A glacier is therefore a reservoir that fills in winter and releases in summer without any engineering, and whose release is **negatively correlated** with rainfall. ⚠️ That negative correlation is the whole value of the service, and it is why the loss of the store is a **reliability** problem rather than a **volume** problem.

### 3. The peak-water logic

⚠️ The phased response is recorded by the Core owner in its current anchor: **meltwater may first increase, then decline after substantial ice loss.**

❗ The reasoning, in three steps:

1. ⚠️ As warming raises the equilibrium line, more of the glacier's area lies in the ablation zone and annual melt **rises**. Runoff increases.
2. ⚠️ The increase is being financed by **drawing down the stock**. The glacier is releasing water accumulated over decades or centuries.
3. ⚠️ As the stock shrinks, the area available to melt shrinks with it, and annual melt **falls** — eventually below its original level, because the store that buffered the lean season is gone.

❗ Two examination consequences. ⚠️ **First**, an observed increase in glacier-fed flow is **not** reassurance; it is the first half of a decline, and a candidate who says so has demonstrated the strongest single piece of analysis available in this topic. ⚠️ **Second**, the timing of the peak is **glacier-specific and basin-specific**, depending on glacier size, hypsometry, debris cover and the share of melt in total flow. ❗ **No date for peak water in any Indian basin is asserted anywhere in this file**, because no source available to this edition supplies one.

### 4. Basin differentiation, and the ordering that runs the wrong way

⚠️ The decisive analytical move in this lesson is to notice that **two basin orderings exist and they are not the same ordering**.

| ⚠️ Basin | ✅ Mean snout retreat (Ministry of Earth Sciences, 31 July 2025) | ⚠️ Glacier-melt share of flow |
|---|---|---|
| **Indus** | **12.7 ± 13.2 m/a** — the **slowest** | ⚠️ The **highest** of the three: an arid to semi-arid basin with limited monsoon penetration, where snow and ice melt supply a large share of flow |
| **Ganga** | **15.5 ± 14.4 m/a** | ⚠️ Intermediate: strongly monsoonal, with a large rain-fed contribution from a vast non-glacierised catchment |
| **Brahmaputra** | **20.2 ± 19.7 m/a** — the **fastest** | ⚠️ The **lowest** relative dependence: among the wettest catchments on Earth, where monsoon rainfall dominates the annual total |

❗ **The ordering of retreat is close to the reverse of the ordering of dependence.** ⚠️ The basin losing ice fastest is the one that needs it least, in relative terms, and the basin losing it slowest is the one most dependent on it. ❗ A candidate who states that asymmetry has produced a genuinely analytical sentence, and it is the kind of observation that distinguishes a nine from a six.

⚠️ **Two cautions travel with it, and both must be written.** ❗ First, all three retreat means carry uncertainties larger than themselves, so the retreat ordering is real but **not cleanly separated** (Lesson 14). ❗ Second, **no source available to this edition quantifies the glacier-melt share of flow for any Indian basin**; the dependence ordering above is an ⚠️ inference from basin aridity and monsoon geography, and it is marked as such. ⚠️ Write it as reasoning — *"the Indus is the most glacier-dependent of the three because it is the least monsoon-fed"* — and not as a statistic.

### 5. The pathways, in descending order of certainty

⚠️ The Core owner's step four is an ordering instruction as much as a list, and the order is by **certainty**, not by severity.

| ⚠️ Pathway | ⚠️ Mechanism | ⚠️ Certainty and limitation |
|---|---|---|
| **(a) Dry-season flow reliability** | Loss of the seasonal store removes the lean-season and drought-year buffer | ⚠️ Most certain, because it follows directly from the storage mechanism. ❗ But its magnitude depends on the melt share, which is unquantified here |
| **(b) Glacial lakes and outburst exposure** | ✅ Retreat ponds meltwater behind moraine; the Ministry of Earth Sciences names **lake formation and enlargement, enhancing flash-flood and outburst frequency**, as its second pathway | ⚠️ Well attested, and developed at Lessons 16 and 17. ❗ Exposure, not only hazard, drives the risk |
| **(c) Slope instability and sediment flux** | ✅ The Core owner records **loss of ice support and permafrost thaw in rock walls** producing more rockfall and debris flow, and **newly exposed moraine** as easily mobilised sediment | ⚠️ Attested; 🔁 **slope-failure mechanics belong to the weathering and mass-movement owner** and are named, not taught |
| **(d) Sea-level contribution** | ✅ The Ministry of Earth Sciences names **rising sea level globally** as its third pathway; grounded ice transfers mass to the ocean (Lesson 4) | ⚠️ Real but **globally additive and dominated by polar ice**. ❗ The weakest pathway for an Indian water answer; 🔁 coastal consequences belong to the coastal-landforms owner |

⚠️ The Core owner adds a fifth consequence that belongs in a complete answer: ✅ **albedo feedback** — bright ice replaced by darker rock or water absorbs more radiation, amplifying local and regional warming. 🔁 Regional climate amplification belongs to the polar-climate and climate owners and is named, not developed.

### 6. The balancing point — the branch that prevents the exaggeration

❗ ✅ **"Monsoon rainfall, not melt, supplies the bulk of annual flow in most of these basins, so the melt contribution matters disproportionately in the lean season and in drought years rather than in the annual total. Saying this prevents the common exaggeration."** That is the Core owner's step five, and it is the single most valuable sentence in the lesson.

⚠️ Why it is worth so much:

1. ⚠️ It **pre-empts the examiner's objection**. The strongest criticism of a glacier-water answer is that it overstates the hydrological role of ice in a monsoon country; an answer that raises and answers that criticism cannot be marked down for it.
2. ⚠️ It **sharpens the claim rather than weakening it**. Conceding the annual total and asserting the lean season converts a vague threat into a specific, defensible one: a **timing and reliability risk**.
3. ⚠️ It **connects to policy without crossing into it**. If the risk is about timing, the responses are storage, demand management and cropping choice — 🔁 all of which belong to the water-resources, economy and environment owners and are **named and routed**, not developed here.

❗ The sentence to carry: ⚠️ *"Because the monsoon supplies the bulk of annual flow in these basins, the loss of glacier storage is not primarily a loss of water volume; it is a loss of flow reliability in exactly the months and the years when rainfall fails."*

### 7. 🇮🇳 India hook — the Chandra basin as a worked case, and the limitation

🇮🇳 📰 ✅ The best-documented Indian case is the **Chandra basin in Himachal Pradesh**, where the National Centre for Polar and Ocean Research has monitored glaciers since **2013**. ✅ The Ministry of Earth Sciences records that the basin covers **2,437 square kilometres**, that it has **lost about 6 per cent of its glacial area in the last twenty years**, and that annual retreat over the last decade has run at **13 to 33 metres a year**, with the **Himansh** field research station operational since **2016**.

🇮🇳 ⚠️ The Chandra is the headstream of the **Chenab** (Lesson 13), fed by Bara Shigri, Sonapani and Chhota Shigri among others, and the Chenab is an Indus-system river. ⚠️ The basin therefore sits in the part of the Himalaya where the melt share of flow is highest and the monsoon penetration is weakest — which makes it the **most informative Indian basin for the storage argument**, and not coincidentally the one the government has instrumented most heavily.

❗ **The limitation, stated with the hook.** ⚠️ **Six per cent of area over twenty years is an area figure, not a mass figure**, and Lesson 14 established that a glacier can lose far more mass than area by thinning. ⚠️ The Ministry's own cumulative mass figure — **−26 metres water equivalent over 1975-2023 for Indian Himalayan glaciers** — is the correct quantity when a loss magnitude is needed. ❗ And **no runoff projection, no peak-water date and no water-supply figure for the Chenab or any other Indian basin is asserted anywhere in this file**, because no source available to this edition supplies one. ⚠️ The argument here is about **mechanism and direction**, and it should be written that way.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| Pair | The discriminator | The wrong answer it prevents |
|---|---|---|
| **Water volume / water timing** | **Annual total** against **lean-season reliability** | "Himalayan rivers will dry up" |
| **Peak water rising / water security improving** | **Stock drawdown** against **sustainable yield** | Reading increased melt flow as reassurance |
| **Retreat ordering / dependence ordering** | **Brahmaputra fastest** against **Indus most dependent** | Assuming the fastest-retreating basin is the most threatened |
| **Supply-side change / basin water stress** | **Melt contribution** against **demand, storage and allocation** | Attributing Indian water stress to glacier retreat |
| **Hazard / exposure** | **Lake growth** against **valley-floor development** | Treating outburst risk as purely cryospheric |

**Strongest objection.** If the monsoon supplies most of the flow, glacier retreat is a second-order hydrological issue, and an answer that concedes this has conceded the question. The topic's water-security claim is therefore overstated.

**Reply.** The concession is about the **annual total**, and the claim was never about the annual total. ⚠️ Three replies, in ascending order of force. ❗ **First**, a water system's reliability is determined by its **worst** periods, not its average ones; a basin whose mean annual flow is unchanged but whose lean-season minimum falls is a basin with a new constraint on irrigation, hydropower and drinking supply. ❗ **Second**, the buffer matters most in **drought years**, when the monsoon under-performs and melt is simultaneously enhanced by warm, clear conditions — so the store is at its most valuable precisely when the alternative source has failed, and losing it removes a **negatively correlated** supply. ❗ **Third**, the melt share is **not uniform**: it is highest in the arid, weakly monsoonal upper Indus system, where the concession has least force. ⚠️ The honest conclusion is therefore graded, which is what the Core owner's step seven requires: **a timing-and-reliability risk, concentrated in particular basins and particular seasons, within a supply system whose annual total is monsoon-dominated.**

⚠️ **Residual.** The **melt share of flow in Indian basins is not quantified by any source available to this edition**, nor is the timing of peak water for any Indian glacier or basin. ❗ Both are therefore treated as mechanisms with stated directions, never as numbers.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Salient features of world's physical geography" (Mains General Studies Paper I, direct ownership). ⚠️ The demand family this lesson serves is the *changes in critical geographical features, including ice-caps, and the effects of such changes* family.
- **Verified linkage:** ❗ this lesson **carries the owned 2020 General Studies Paper I Question 6 demand — melting Himalayan glaciers and India's water resources, *how will it impact*, 10 marks, 150 words**. ❗ No solved previous-year answer appears in this file; what follows is an **approach**. **The approach:** the directive is *how will it impact*, so the answer must be a **chain of consequences**, not a description of melting. Compress the seven-step spine to roughly 150 words: one sentence of mechanism (mass balance, lagged and heterogeneous response); one sentence of the **storage thesis**; then the impact chain in descending certainty — **lean-season and drought-year flow reliability**, then **lake growth and outburst exposure**, then **slope instability and sediment**, then **sea-level contribution** attributed to the Ministry of Earth Sciences; then the **peak-water** logic in one clause — more first, then less; then the **balancing point** — the monsoon supplies the bulk of annual flow, so the risk is one of timing and reliability; and a graded conclusion. ❗ Attach at most one or two dated figures with their agency, and prefer the **cumulative −26 metres water equivalent, 1975-2023** and the **basin-ordered retreat means with their uncertainties**. ❗ Do not quote a melt share, a peak-water date or a runoff projection.
- **Probable framing:** as an impact-chain demand; as a fifteen-mark *"glacier change and its effects"* demand, for which the full seven-step spine is already the plan; or as a clause inside a broader Himalayan or water-security question.
- **Traps:** predicting that Himalayan rivers will dry up; treating increased melt flow as reassurance; quoting an unsourced melt percentage; assuming the fastest-retreating basin is the most dependent; attributing Indian water stress wholly to glacier retreat; leading with the sea-level pathway; developing institutional response doctrine, which is routed.
- **Answer use:** the reusable opening is *"Himalayan glaciers matter less as a volume of water than as a seasonal store that releases in the months when rainfall is least, so their loss is a reliability risk rather than a volume risk."*

Revision notes:

- 🔁 ⚠️ This lesson is **Lesson 2's ledger and Lesson 4's grounded-ice rule applied to water resources**; step two of the spine *is* Lesson 2.
- ✅ **Seven-step spine:** thesis (**seasonal storage service**) → mechanism (mass balance, lag) → **complication** (debris, size, thinning ≠ retreat, heterogeneity) → effects in descending certainty → **balance** (monsoon, not melt) → response → **graded conclusion**.
- ⚠️ **Why the store matters:** melt is concentrated in warm, dry, clear conditions — **negatively correlated with rainfall**. The service is **timing**, not volume.
- ⚠️ **Peak water:** more runoff first (stock drawdown), then less. ❗ **An increase in flow is the first half of a decline, not reassurance.** ❗ No peak-water date is asserted.
- ✅ **Retreat ordering (Ministry of Earth Sciences, 31 July 2025): Brahmaputra 20.2 ± 19.7 > Ganga 15.5 ± 14.4 > Indus 12.7 ± 13.2 m/a.**
- ⚠️ **Dependence ordering runs the other way: Indus > Ganga > Brahmaputra**, because the Indus system is least monsoon-fed. ❗ This is **inference**, not a quoted statistic — no melt share is available in this lane.
- ✅ **Four pathways in descending certainty:** dry-season flow reliability · **lake formation and enlargement, enhancing outburst frequency** · slope instability and sediment flux · **rising sea level globally**. ✅ Plus **albedo feedback** (Core owner).
- ❗ ✅ **The balancing point:** *"monsoon rainfall, not melt, supplies the bulk of annual flow in most of these basins"*, so melt matters **disproportionately in the lean season and in drought years**.
- ⚠️ Its three uses: **pre-empts the examiner's objection · sharpens the claim · connects to policy without crossing into it**.
- 🇮🇳 📰 ✅ **Chandra basin:** NCPOR monitoring since **2013**; area **2,437 km²**; **~6 per cent of glacial area lost in 20 years**; retreat **13-33 m/yr** over the last decade; **Himansh** since **2016**. ⚠️ Chandra → Chenab → Indus system, the most melt-dependent sector.
- ❗ Six per cent is an **area** figure; the mass figure is **−26 m w.e., 1975-2023**.
- 🔁 **Routed:** slope-failure mechanics → weathering and mass-movement owner; coastal consequences → coastal-landforms owner; regional climate amplification → climate and polar owners; water policy, storage, hydropower finance and demand management → water, economy and environment owners; institutional response doctrine → disaster-management owner.
- ❗ **Graded conclusion:** the water risk is a **timing and reliability** risk; the hazard risk is a **joint product of cryospheric change and valley-floor development**.

### Concept check

**Question:** A newspaper reports that discharge in a glacier-fed Himalayan headstream has risen by a fifth over the last two decades, and concludes that fears about glacier retreat and water security have been exaggerated. Assess the conclusion, and state what further information would be needed to evaluate the basin's position.

**Model answer:** The conclusion inverts the evidence. Rising discharge from a retreating glacier is the **expected first phase**, not a refutation of concern. As the equilibrium line rises, a larger share of the glacier's area lies in the ablation zone and annual melt increases — but that increase is financed by **drawing down the stock**, releasing water accumulated over decades or centuries. As the ice that supplies the melt is consumed, the melting area shrinks and annual melt falls, eventually below its original level, because the store that buffered the lean season has gone. An increase is therefore **the first half of a decline**. Two further points follow. First, the quantity that matters is not the annual total but the **seasonal distribution**: glacier melt is concentrated in warm, dry, clear conditions, which is to say when rainfall is least, so the service being lost is **reliability in the lean season and in drought years**, and an annual figure conceals it. Second, the monsoon supplies the bulk of annual flow in most of these basins, so neither the rise nor the eventual fall should be read as a change in total water availability. To evaluate the basin's position one would need: the **melt share of flow** and its seasonal profile; **mass balance** rather than discharge, since discharge reports the release of a stock rather than its size; the **glacier area and hypsometry** of the catchment, which govern how long the enhanced melt can continue; and the **demand and storage** position of the basin, because water stress is driven by demand and storage as much as by supply.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat a discharge record as a measure of glacier health. Discharge reports what is leaving the store, and a store being rapidly emptied yields more water in the short run than one in balance — which is why mass balance, not runoff, is the diagnostic quantity.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "The loss of Himalayan glaciers is a problem of when water arrives, not of how much." Critically examine. *(Critically examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The proposition is largely sound. Glaciers act as a seasonal store, accumulating snow in winter and releasing meltwater in warm, dry, clear conditions — when rainfall is least and demand is greatest. Their loss therefore removes a supply that is negatively correlated with rainfall, and the governing hydrological point is that monsoon rainfall, not melt, supplies the bulk of annual flow in these basins. The risk is therefore one of lean-season and drought-year reliability rather than annual volume, and peak water sharpens it: runoff rises first as the stock is drawn down, then falls below its former level. Two qualifications are needed. The melt share is not uniform, being highest in the weakly monsoonal upper Indus system, where the volume question has more force. And basin water stress is driven by demand, storage and allocation as much as by supply, so a supply-side account is incomplete on its own.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for the seasonal-store mechanism with the negative correlation to rainfall stated; 2 for the peak-water sequence; 2 for the monsoon balancing point quoted as the reason the claim holds; 1 for the basin-differentiated qualification naming the upper Indus system as the exception; 2 for the demand-and-storage qualification. Deduct 2 for predicting that Himalayan rivers will dry up and 1 for quoting an unsourced melt percentage.

### 1. The Arc-A mechanism, applied

🔁 ⚠️ **This is Lesson 6's cirque rock lip, Lesson 7's over-deepened rock basin, Lesson 8's moraine family and Lesson 9's buried-ice mechanism, assembled into a single classification.** ❗ A glacial lake is not a new landform; it is **water occupying a basin that Arc A already explained**, held back by a dam that Arc A already described. ⚠️ And because Arc A established what each dam is made of, the hazard ranking of Lesson 17 follows without new physics.

### 2. The organising question, and why it is better than a list

⚠️ Glacial lakes are commonly taught as a list of seven types. ❗ A list is almost useless, because the types overlap: a proglacial lake may be moraine-dammed, a supraglacial pond may coalesce into a large lake, and a thermokarst hollow may fill and merge with a proglacial body.

❗ **The question "what dams it?" is better for three reasons.** ⚠️ It is **answerable from a photograph or an image**; it **predicts stability**, because dam material determines failure mode; and it **ranks hazard**, because a rock dam does not fail while an unconsolidated debris dam containing buried ice can fail catastrophically. ⚠️ A candidate who opens a glacial-lake answer with the dam question has organised the material and begun the hazard argument in the same sentence.

### 3. The types, with what each one contributes

| ⚠️ Type | ⚠️ How it forms | ⚠️ Dam | ⚠️ Hazard significance |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Cirque or tarn** | Water in the over-deepened cirque basin behind its rock lip (Lesson 6) | **Rock** | ❗ Low. A rock dam does not fail |
| **Erosional bedrock basin / ribbon lake** | Water in an over-deepened reach of the trough (Lesson 7) | **Rock**, sometimes with a moraine at the lower end | Low, unless the moraine is the effective dam |
| **Moraine-dammed** | Meltwater impounded behind a terminal or lateral moraine | **Unconsolidated, unsorted till, often with buried ice** | ❗ **High. This is the outburst reservoir** |
| **Proglacial** | In the basin immediately in front of the retreating snout | **Varies** — rock, moraine or both | ⚠️ Depends entirely on the dam; the term describes position |
| **Ice-dammed** | A glacier blocks a tributary or trunk valley | **Ice** | ❗ High, and characteristically **recurrent**: ice dams fail and reform |
| **Supraglacial** | Ponds in hollows on a debris-covered tongue | **Ice and debris** | ⚠️ Individually small, but they coalesce; they also concentrate melt |
| **Thermokarst** | Collapse where buried or ground ice melts out (Lesson 9's kettle mechanism) | **Sediment** | ⚠️ Generally small; indicative of degrading ice |

### 4. The verified inventories, each with its threshold and date

❗ The Core owner's factual-caution box forbids quoting a lake inventory from memory. ⚠️ The following are quoted with their issuing body, their document and their threshold.

📰 ✅ **National Remote Sensing Centre, *Glacial Lake Atlas of Indian River Basins*, 2023**, as tabulated by the Central Water Commission in its *Monthly Monitoring Report of Glacial Lakes and Water Bodies, August 2025*:

| ✅ Size class (hectares) | ✅ In India | ✅ Transboundary | ✅ Total |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| 0.25 to 1 | **3,342** | **9,194** | **12,536** |
| 1 to 5 | **2,862** | **7,769** | **10,631** |
| 5 to 10 | **712** | **1,733** | **2,445** |
| 10 to 50 | **596** | **1,536** | **2,132** |
| above 50 | **58** | **241** | **299** |
| **Total** | **7,570** | **20,473** | **28,043** |

❗ **Read three things off that table, not one.** ⚠️ **First**, the threshold: the atlas maps lakes **of 0.25 hectares and above**, and changing that threshold would change the total dramatically — more than 12,000 of the 28,043 lie in the smallest class. ⚠️ **Second**, the boundary: **only 7,570 of the 28,043 lie in India**, and the remaining 20,473 are transboundary, which has direct consequences for monitoring and for warning. ⚠️ **Third**, the distribution: lakes **above 50 hectares number only 299 in total and 58 in India**, so the population that matters most for large-volume outburst is small enough to be monitored individually.

📰 ✅ **Central Water Commission monitoring universe, August 2025 report:** **2,843** glacial lakes and water bodies above 10 hectares are monitored, comprising **902 legacy** — 477 above 50 hectares from the 2011 inventory, monitored since 2011, plus **425 of 10 to 50 hectares added in 2022**, of which 385 came from the 2011 inventory and **40 were high-priority lakes identified by the Swiss Agency for Development and Cooperation for the National Disaster Management Authority** — **plus 1,941 added in 2025 from the 2023 atlas**, of which **581 are within India** and 1,360 transboundary.

📰 ✅ **Ministry of Jal Shakti, 8 August 2024:** as of that date the Central Water Commission monitored **902 glacial lakes and water bodies** — **477 above 50 hectares and 425 of 10 to 50 hectares** — **from June to October every year**.

❗ **The cohort warning, and it is mandatory.** ⚠️ The monitored population changed from **477** to **902** to **2,843** in the fetched record. ❗ **That is a change in how many lakes are watched, not in how many exist.** ⚠️ Quote any Central Water Commission figure **with its cohort, its base year and its report month**, and never compare across cohorts.

### 5. How the monitoring is actually done, and why the method matters

📰 ✅ The August 2025 report states the method, and the date-and-data control requires that a figure be reported with it.

- ✅ **Sensors: Sentinel-2 multispectral and Sentinel-1 synthetic aperture radar at 10-metre resolution**, processed in **Google Earth Engine** with an in-house automatic algorithm plus manual digitisation. 🔁 Remote-sensing technology itself belongs to the science-and-technology owner; it is named here because the date-and-data control requires the method behind a figure.
- ✅ **The 2011 inventory's provenance:** published in **August 2011** by the Central Water Commission with the National Remote Sensing Centre, Hyderabad, from **AWiFS and Resourcesat-1** data collected **May to November 2009**.
- ✅ **Classification scheme, five classes:** increase above 40 per cent · increase up to 40 per cent · no change · decrease · not analysed. ❗ **Lakes showing an increase above 40 per cent are identified as requiring vigorous monitoring for disaster purposes.**
- ✅ **Change-detection baselines:** current area against the **base year 2011**, against the **five-year average 2020-2024**, and against the **ten-year average 2015-2024** — and **the minimum of the three is adopted**.
- ❗ ✅ **Stated limitations, quoted because they are a first-party admission:** automatic mapping is degraded by **mountain shadow, snow cover, cloud cover, partly frozen lakes and dried-up lakes**, and **some lakes cannot be mapped at all in a given month**.

⚠️ The limitations paragraph is worth as much as the inventory. ❗ It tells a candidate that an **unmapped lake in a monthly report is not an absent lake**, and that a satellite-derived area carries conditions of observation — exactly the discipline Lesson 14 established for retreat rates.

### 6. Lake growth as a signal, with two named Indian cases

📰 ✅ The Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change recorded on **25 July 2024** that **Samudra Tapu and Gepang Gath**, both in the Chandra Basin, *"have shown substantial expansion in their area and volume over the last five decades (1971-2022), which is significant in terms of their hazard potential."*

⚠️ The link to Lesson 13 and Lesson 14 is direct and worth stating in an answer: ✅ the same two lakes' parent glaciers appear in the Ministry of Jal Shakti's Annexure-II with snout retreat rates — **Gepang Gath 45.0 metres a year, 2003-2012** and **Samundar Tapu 24.8 metres a year, 1962-2013**, both in the Chenab basin. ❗ **Retreat and lake expansion are documented for the same features by two different ministries**, which is as close to a closed causal demonstration as this evidence base provides. ⚠️ It is still a correlation of two monitored quantities rather than an attribution of the expansion to any single cause, and it should be written that way.

📰 ✅ A third documented case: the Ministry of Jal Shakti's answer of 20 March 2025 records that the Uttarakhand State Disaster Management Authority, with the Wadia Institute of Himalayan Geology, is assessing **13 glacial lakes** across Pithoragarh, Chamoli, Tehri, Uttarkashi and Bageshwar, and that **Vasundhara Tal** — a proglacial lake at the terminus of the **Raykana Glacier** in the Dhauliganga basin, Chamoli — **expanded from 0.14 square kilometres in 1968 to 0.59 square kilometres in 2021**, with a **bathymetric survey in 2024**.

✅ Finally, the Ministry of Jal Shakti recorded on **8 August 2024** that the **National Centre for Polar and Ocean Research has monitored two pro-glacial lakes in the Chandra Basin since 2013**.

### 7. 🇮🇳 India hook — a transboundary inventory, and the boundary with the lakes owner

🇮🇳 ⚠️ The most consequential single fact in the inventory is political rather than glaciological: ✅ **of 28,043 mapped glacial lakes in Indian river basins, only 7,570 lie within India and 20,473 are transboundary.** ⚠️ That means the majority of the lakes whose failure would send a flood wave into Indian territory are **not on Indian ground**, which is why satellite monitoring rather than field instrumentation is the only feasible inventory method for most of them, and why the warning problem at Lesson 18 is partly an information problem rather than an engineering one.

❗ **The limitation, stated with the hook.** ⚠️ **A mapped lake is not a dangerous lake.** ❗ The atlas is an inventory of **existence**, classified by size; the Central Water Commission's monitoring adds a **change** classification; neither is a hazard ranking, and the Core owner's caution box explicitly forbids quoting an inventory of "potentially dangerous" lakes. ⚠️ The honest statement is that **28,043 lakes are mapped, 2,843 above 10 hectares are monitored monthly from June to October, and lakes expanding by more than 40 per cent are flagged for vigorous monitoring** — none of which is the same as a count of dangerous lakes.

```text
   +==========================================================================+
   |  BOUNDARY NOTE - THE LAKES AND WETLANDS OWNER                           |
   |  General lake and wetland classification, limnology, eutrophication,    |
   |  Ramsar designation and lake shrinkage belong to the lakes-and-wetlands |
   |  owner. This topic owns ONLY lakes that are GLACIALLY FORMED or         |
   |  GLACIER-DAMMED, and it owns them as the HAZARD RESERVOIR of the        |
   |  outburst chain. No general limnology is taught here.                   |
   +==========================================================================+
```

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| Pair | The discriminator | The wrong answer it prevents |
|---|---|---|
| **Tarn / moraine-dammed lake** | **Rock dam** against **unconsolidated till dam with buried ice** | Treating all glacial lakes as equally hazardous |
| **Proglacial / moraine-dammed** | **Position** against **dam type** | Assuming every proglacial lake has a weak dam |
| **Ice-dammed / moraine-dammed** | **Ice** against **debris**; ice dams fail **recurrently** | Treating every failure as a once-only event |
| **Mapped lake / monitored lake** | **28,043 mapped** against **2,843 monitored above 10 hectares** | Quoting the atlas total as a monitoring figure |
| **Monitored lake / dangerous lake** | **Inventory and change class** against **hazard ranking** | "There are 28,043 dangerous lakes" |
| **Cohort growth / lake proliferation** | **477 → 902 → 2,843 watched** against lakes existing | Reading the monitoring record as a hazard trend |

**Strongest objection.** The inventory numbers are a monitoring statistic, not geography, and they will be out of date within a year. A Mains answer should stay with the mechanism and leave the counting to agencies.

**Reply.** The mechanism alone cannot answer the question an examiner actually asks, which is about **scale and exposure**. ⚠️ "Retreat creates lakes" is a mechanism; *"the National Remote Sensing Centre's 2023 atlas maps 28,043 glacial lakes of 0.25 hectares and above in Indian river basins, of which 7,570 lie in India and 20,473 are transboundary, and the Central Water Commission monitored 2,843 above 10 hectares in 2025"* is the same mechanism **with its magnitude, its threshold, its boundary and its date**. ❗ The second is not more perishable than the first; it is **self-dating**, and therefore cannot be falsified by a newer atlas. ⚠️ And the transboundary split is not a monitoring statistic at all — it is a geographical fact with direct consequences for how the hazard can be managed.

⚠️ **Residual.** **Lake volume is not inventoried.** The atlas classifies by **area**, and area is a poor proxy for the volume that would be released in a breach, because depth varies greatly and bathymetry exists for very few lakes — ✅ Vasundhara Tal's 2024 bathymetric survey is recorded precisely because such surveys are exceptional. ❗ No outburst volume is asserted anywhere in this file.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Salient features of world's physical geography" (Mains General Studies Paper I, direct ownership). ⚠️ The demand family served is the *changes in critical geographical features, including water-bodies, and the effects of such changes* family.
- **Verified linkage:** no owned demand sits here. ⚠️ This lesson is the **reservoir half** of the outburst argument that Lesson 17 completes, and it supplies the second effect pathway of the owned 2020 demand carried at Lesson 15. 🔁 **General lake and wetland classification is routed to the lakes-and-wetlands owner.**
- **Probable framing:** as a classification-with-hazard-implication demand; as the **scale paragraph** of an outburst answer; or as evidence in a cryosphere-change answer.
- **Traps:** treating all glacial lakes as equally hazardous; quoting the atlas total as a monitoring figure or as a count of dangerous lakes; comparing monitoring cohorts; omitting the 0.25-hectare threshold; omitting the transboundary split; inferring volume from area; drifting into general limnology.
- **Answer use:** the reusable opening is *"A glacial lake should be classified by what dams it, because the dam decides the failure mode: a rock lip does not fail, while a terminal moraine of unconsolidated till containing buried ice can."*

Revision notes:

- 🔁 ⚠️ This lesson assembles **Lesson 6's rock lip, Lesson 7's over-deepened basin, Lesson 8's moraine and Lesson 9's buried ice** into one key.
- ❗ **The organising question is "what dams it?"** — it is answerable from an image, it predicts stability, and it ranks hazard.
- ⚠️ **Seven types:** cirque or tarn (**rock**) · erosional bedrock basin and ribbon lake (**rock**) · **moraine-dammed (unconsolidated till with buried ice — the outburst reservoir)** · proglacial (**position, not dam type**) · ice-dammed (**ice; recurrent failure**) · supraglacial (**coalescing ponds**) · thermokarst (**buried-ice collapse**).
- ⚠️ **Retreat manufactures lakes:** vacated over-deepened basin plus an impounding moraine already in place → meltwater ponds → lake grows → **area is satellite-measurable, so growth is a monitorable signal**.
- ✅ **NRSC *Glacial Lake Atlas of Indian River Basins*, 2023: 28,043 glacial lakes ≥ 0.25 ha — 7,570 in India, 20,473 transboundary.** By size: **0.25-1 ha: 12,536 · 1-5: 10,631 · 5-10: 2,445 · 10-50: 2,132 · above 50: 299 (58 in India)**.
- ✅ **CWC, August 2025: 2,843 lakes and water bodies above 10 ha monitored** = **902 legacy** (477 above 50 ha since 2011; **425 of 10-50 ha added 2022**, including **40 high-priority lakes identified by the Swiss Agency for Development and Cooperation for the NDMA**) **plus 1,941 added in 2025** from the 2023 atlas (**581 in India**, 1,360 transboundary).
- ✅ **Ministry of Jal Shakti, 8 August 2024: 902 monitored — 477 above 50 ha and 425 of 10-50 ha — from June to October every year.**
- ❗ **477 → 902 → 2,843 is a change in the COHORT WATCHED, not in the number of lakes.** Quote cohort, base year and report month.
- ✅ **Method: Sentinel-2 multispectral and Sentinel-1 SAR at 10 m**, in **Google Earth Engine**, automatic algorithm plus manual digitisation. ✅ The **2011 inventory** came from **AWiFS and Resourcesat-1**, data of **May-November 2009**, published **August 2011**.
- ✅ **Five change classes**; ❗ **increase above 40 per cent → vigorous monitoring for disaster purposes**. ✅ Baselines: **2011 base year, 2020-2024 five-year mean, 2015-2024 ten-year mean — the minimum of the three is adopted**.
- ❗ ✅ **Stated limitations:** mountain shadow, snow cover, cloud cover, partly frozen lakes and dried-up lakes degrade mapping; **some lakes cannot be mapped at all in a given month**.
- 📰 ✅ **Samudra Tapu and Gepang Gath** (Chandra Basin) show *"substantial expansion in their area and volume over the last five decades (1971-2022)"*; ✅ their parent glaciers appear in Annexure-II at **45.0 m/yr (2003-2012)** and **24.8 m/yr (1962-2013)**.
- 📰 ✅ **Vasundhara Tal** (Raykana Glacier terminus, Dhauliganga basin, Chamoli): **0.14 km² in 1968 → 0.59 km² in 2021**, bathymetric survey **2024**; **13 Uttarakhand lakes** under assessment by the state authority with the Wadia Institute.
- ❗ **A mapped lake is not a dangerous lake.** ❗ **Area is not volume**; bathymetry exists for very few lakes.
- 🔁 **General lake and wetland classification, limnology, eutrophication, Ramsar and lake shrinkage → lakes-and-wetlands owner.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Two lakes sit a kilometre apart in the same glaciated valley. Lake P occupies a circular basin at the head of a side valley, retained by a rock threshold. Lake Q lies immediately in front of a retreating glacier snout, retained by an arcuate ridge of mixed boulders, gravel and clay. Classify both, rank their hazard, and state what further information would be required before either could be called dangerous.

**Model answer:** **Lake P is a tarn**, occupying an erosional cirque basin over-deepened by rotational abrasion and retained by the rock lip that survived at the outlet where the ice was thinner. **Lake Q is a moraine-dammed proglacial lake**: proglacial by position, since it lies in the basin the retreating snout has vacated, and moraine-dammed by mechanism, since the arcuate ridge of mixed, unsorted debris is a terminal moraine. **The hazard ranking follows from the dam material and is unambiguous.** Lake P's dam is **bedrock**, which does not fail; its level can change but its basin cannot be breached. Lake Q's dam is **unconsolidated, unsorted till**, deposited by an agent that never compacted it, very commonly containing **buried ice** that melts out over time and weakens the structure from within — exactly the kettle mechanism seen in harmless form in outwash. Such a dam can fail by overtopping, by seepage and internal erosion, or by mechanical collapse. **Before either lake could be called dangerous, however, several further things would be needed**: the dam's geometry and freeboard; whether buried ice is present and how much; the slopes above the lake, since a rock or ice avalanche could generate a displacement wave; the lake's **volume**, which area alone does not give, since bathymetry exists for very few lakes; the catchment's inflow regime; the valley geometry downstream; and above all the **exposure** downstream — settlements, roads, bridges and infrastructure in the flood path. Hazard is a property of the lake and its dam; risk requires the exposure term as well.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat a mapped or monitored lake as a dangerous one. National inventories classify by area and by area change, and a monitoring list that grows from 477 to 902 to 2,843 records an expansion of surveillance rather than a proliferation of hazards.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** Explain how glacier retreat creates glacial lakes, and why the resulting lakes differ so greatly in the risk they pose. *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** Retreat creates lakes because it vacates basins the ice excavated and leaves in place the ridges it deposited. As the snout withdraws up an over-deepened trough or cirque, meltwater ponds in the hollow, and where a terminal or lateral moraine lies across the valley it impounds the water behind it. Satellite imagery can measure the resulting area, so lake growth becomes a monitorable signal of retreat. The risk differs because the dam differs. A tarn or rock-basin lake is retained by bedrock and does not fail. A moraine-dammed lake is retained by unconsolidated, unsorted till, never compacted and frequently containing buried ice, so it can fail by overtopping, internal erosion or collapse. An ice-dammed lake can fail recurrently as the ice dam floats or drains. The qualification is that hazard is not risk: exposure downstream, dam geometry, slope stability above and lake volume must all be assessed.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for the vacated-basin plus in-place-moraine mechanism explained rather than asserted; 1 for lake growth identified as a measurable monitoring signal; 3 for dam material used to differentiate failure modes across at least three lake types; 2 for buried ice named as an internal weakening mechanism with at least two failure modes given; 1 for the hazard-is-not-risk qualification. Deduct 2 for treating all glacial lakes as equally hazardous.

### 1. The Arc-A mechanism, applied

🔁 ⚠️ **This is Lesson 8's moraine material and Lesson 9's buried-ice mechanism, applied to a dam.** ❗ The reason a moraine dam is weak is not a separate fact to memorise: it is a **direct consequence of how the material was deposited**. ⚠️ Ice cannot sort, so the ridge is a chaotic mixture of boulders, gravel, sand and clay; ice does not compact what it drops, so the material is loose; and ice is frequently buried within the ridge, so the structure **loses volume and strength over time as that ice melts out** — exactly the kettle-hole mechanism of Lesson 9, operating in a place where it matters.

### 2. The hazard lane, step by step

⚠️ The Core owner's chain is the spine of this lesson and is worth holding in its own words, because its ordering is the answer structure.

1. ⚠️ **Glacier retreat.** A negative mass balance raises the equilibrium line and the snout withdraws (Lesson 2).
2. ⚠️ **Meltwater ponds** behind the terminal or lateral moraine, or in the basin the ice has vacated.
3. ⚠️ **A proglacial or moraine-dammed lake grows** (Lesson 16), and the growth is measurable from orbit.
4. ❗ **The dam is unconsolidated debris, often with buried ice, and is inherently weak.**
5. ⚠️ **A trigger arrives:** rock or ice avalanche into the lake, extreme rainfall, dam seepage and piping, earthquake, or upstream lake failure in cascade.
6. ⚠️ **Sudden breach.**
7. ⚠️ **A high-energy, sediment-charged flood wave** travels far down a steep valley, scouring channels and destroying roads, bridges, settlements and hydropower assets.

⚠️ **Three properties of the resulting flood distinguish it from ordinary flooding**, and the Core owner names the error — ❌ *"treat GLOF as ordinary flooding"* — in its directive table.

| ⚠️ Property | ⚠️ Mechanism | ⚠️ Consequence |
|---|---|---|
| **Sudden onset with no meteorological warning** | The release is a dam failure, not a rainfall-runoff response | Conventional flood forecasting based on rainfall and river gauges may give no lead time at all |
| **Sediment-charged** | The wave entrains moraine, channel fill and bank material as it goes | It behaves partly as a debris flow; its destructive power and its deposits exceed those of clear-water flood of the same volume |
| **Confined in a steep, narrow valley** | Mountain valley geometry concentrates the wave rather than spreading it | Depth and velocity remain high far downstream; the wave can scour the channel to bedrock |

### 3. The breach modes, which are not the same as the triggers

❗ A distinction most answers miss: **the trigger is what initiates; the breach mode is how the dam actually fails.** ⚠️ One trigger can produce different breach modes, and a dam can fail without any external trigger at all.

| ⚠️ Breach mode | ⚠️ Mechanism | ⚠️ What governs it |
|---|---|---|
| **Overtopping** | Water passes over the dam crest and erodes it from the downstream face backwards | **Freeboard** — the height of the crest above the water surface — and the inflow or wave that consumes it |
| **Piping and internal erosion** | Seepage through the loose, unsorted dam entrains fine material, enlarging a conduit until the structure collapses | Dam permeability, hydraulic gradient, and the presence of coarse, poorly graded material |
| **Mechanical collapse** | The dam loses strength or volume — classically through the **melting out of buried ice** — and fails under the water load | Buried-ice content and the thermal state of the dam |

❗ ⚠️ The **displacement wave** deserves separate mention because it couples the two lanes. A rock or ice avalanche entering the lake generates a wave that can overtop the dam **without any change in lake volume at all**. ⚠️ That means a lake can be stable in area, stable in level and still fail — which is why **area monitoring alone is not a safety assessment**, and why Lesson 18's first-party admission about prediction is as candid as it is.

🔁 ❗ **The mechanics of the rock and ice avalanches that supply that trigger belong to the weathering and mass-movement owner, and seismic hazard belongs to the earthquake owner.** This topic names them as triggers and stops.

### 5. The South Lhonak case, told only with first-party figures

❗ ✅ The Advanced owner instructs that a **named satellite study** be used when quoting lake area. ⚠️ This edition satisfies that instruction with something better: a **first-party, dated, official series** placed before Parliament.

📰 ✅ **Ministry of Jal Shakti, Lok Sabha Starred Question No. 68, answered 7 December 2023.**

| ✅ Element | ✅ What the answer records |
|---|---|
| **Lake area series** | *"Water Spread area of South Lhonak Lake varied from **100 Ha to 160 Ha** during monthly monitoring period i.e. June-October during **2011-2023**. Maximum area was **169 Ha** and **171 Ha** in **Sept 2022** and **Sept 2023** respectively."* |
| **Early-warning history** | A **water-level sensor developed by C-DAC under the Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology was installed at South Lhonak Lake in 2016** as a pilot; *"due to inclement weather conditions prevailing at those high altitudes the system was **damaged and collapsed after 6 months** of installation. At present, there is **no operational early warning system** installed at South Lhonak Lake."* |
| **New pilot** | A **National Disaster Management Authority pilot project for early-warning systems at two glacial lakes — South Lhonak and Shako Cho, Sikkim** — with technical support from the **Swiss Development Corporation** and logistics from the Government of Sikkim |
| **The event** | *"the flash flood of **3rd October 2023**"*; the **Teesta-III dam, owned by Sikkim Urja Limited, was washed out**; the project's emergency response system was activated, **sirens were sounded**, **Chungthang town** was informed, **about 250 people** at the dam-site colonies were evacuated to higher ground, and downstream projects were informed by telephone |
| **Response** | The **National Disaster Response Force deployed 6 teams in Sikkim and 5 in West Bengal**, from 4 October 2023 to 1 November 2023 |
| **Casualties** | **46 human casualties, including 15 army personnel**, reported by the State Government in the Teesta river basin |
| **Monitoring context** | Of **544 glacial lakes above 10 hectares** then monitored, **100 are in India, of which 41 are in Sikkim including South Lhonak, and 10 are in Arunachal Pradesh** |

❗ **These figures, and no others, are used for South Lhonak anywhere in this file.** ⚠️ No lake area from any secondary compilation, no breach volume, no peak discharge, no inundation area and no revised casualty figure appears, because none is carried by a first-party document available to this edition.

⚠️ **What the series actually teaches, which is more than its numbers.** The lake varied between **100 and 160 hectares** across twelve monitoring seasons, reaching **169 hectares in September 2022** and **171 hectares in September 2023**. ❗ The maximum in the month before the event exceeded the long-run range by a modest margin — ⚠️ which means **area alone did not announce the failure**. ⚠️ That is the empirical form of the point made in §3: a lake can fail through a displacement wave or a dam-condition change that an area series does not capture, and it is precisely why the Central Water Commission's own prediction statement is worded as it is.

### 6. The exposure lane, and where this topic stops

⚠️ The Core owner states the exposure argument directly: the Himalaya is exposed because of **young, steep, seismically active relief; heavy debris supply; monsoon-concentrated rainfall; and rapid downstream development of roads, towns and run-of-river hydropower in narrow valleys** — and the hazard has grown through **both cryospheric change and increased exposure.**

📰 ✅ The exposure lane now has a first-party, quantified instance. The Ministry of Jal Shakti recorded on **8 August 2024** that, *"subsequent to Teesta-III Hydroelectric dam collapse in October, 2023"*, the Central Water Commission decided to **review the design flood of all existing and under-construction dams vulnerable to outburst floods** for a combination of probable maximum flood or standard project flood **and** outburst flood; that **outburst studies have been made mandatory for all new dams planned with glacial lakes in their catchments**; and that the **Central Electricity Authority identified 47 dams — 38 commissioned and 9 under construction — as likely to be affected by outburst floods from glacial lakes in Indian territory**, with **outburst studies completed for 31 projects**, compiled by the National Dam Safety Authority.

❗ ⚠️ **Read that as geography, not as administration.** The finding that 47 dams sit in catchments containing glacial lakes is a statement about **where India has built in relation to where the cryosphere is** — which is the exposure lane made concrete.

### 7. 🇮🇳 India hook — the cascade, and the limitation on reading any single event

🇮🇳 ⚠️ The Core owner names **cascading risk** as the modern point: an outburst can be **initiated by a mass movement rather than by melt alone**, and the flood can trigger further failures downstream — ❗ so a single-cause framing is analytically weak. 📰 ✅ The Ministry of Jal Shakti's answer of 20 March 2025 records that the Wadia Institute of Himalayan Geology's research covers *"the **2013 Kedarnath** disaster, the **2021 Chamoli** disaster, and the **2023 South Lhonak** glacial lake outburst"* — three Indian high-mountain events in twelve years, all involving the interaction of water, ice, rock and infrastructure in steep valleys.

❗ **The limitation, stated with the hook, and it is strict.** ⚠️ **No event narrative, death toll, damage figure or trigger reconstruction beyond what the first-party documents carry is used anywhere in this file.** ❗ For Kedarnath and Chamoli, this edition records only that the Wadia Institute's research covers them, because that is all the fetched record supports. ⚠️ For South Lhonak, the first-party account of 7 December 2023 is used in full and nothing is added to it. ❗ The Advanced owner's statement stands as the status of the science: ✅ *"the exact trigger chain remains a subject of post-event research."*

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| Pair | The discriminator | The wrong answer it prevents |
|---|---|---|
| **Outburst flood / ordinary flood** | **Dam failure with no meteorological precursor, sediment-charged, valley-confined** against a **rainfall-runoff response** | The Core owner's named error of treating an outburst as ordinary flooding |
| **Trigger / breach mode** | **What initiates** against **how the dam fails** | Listing avalanche and overtopping as items of one list |
| **Hazard / risk** | **The lake and its dam** against **hazard multiplied by exposure, vulnerability and warning** | "The lake is big, therefore the risk is high" |
| **Lake growth / imminent failure** | A **measurable area signal** against a **dam-condition and trigger question** | Reading an area series as a forecast |
| **Regional signal / event cause** | **Retreat and lake formation across a region** against **why this dam failed on this day** | "Warming caused this flood" |
| **Cryospheric change / exposure growth** | The **supply of hazard** against the **presence of assets** | Explaining rising losses by climate alone |

**Strongest objection.** If specific outbursts cannot be predicted, the analysis is of no practical use, and an examination answer that ends in an admission of unpredictability has failed to answer the question.

**Reply.** Unpredictability of **timing** is entirely compatible with high usefulness, and the distinction is the heart of hazard geography. ⚠️ What the analysis delivers is not a forecast but four actionable things: ❗ **identification of the population at issue** — which lakes, of what dam type, with what change signal; ❗ **ranking by dam condition and downstream exposure**, which is the only ranking that matters; ❗ **the design implication**, exemplified by the first-party decision to review design floods for a combination of conventional and outburst flood and to make outburst studies mandatory for new dams in glacial-lake catchments; and ❗ **the siting implication**, which is that exposure is the term a society actually controls. ⚠️ The Central Water Commission draws exactly that conclusion in the same paragraph as its admission: *"As direct predictions cannot be made, there is an urgent need to monitor a careful selection of prioritized lakes on a regular basis."* 🔁 How that monitoring is institutionalised is the disaster-management owner's question and is routed.

⚠️ **Residual.** **The trigger chain of the October 2023 Sikkim event is not established** by the documents available to this edition; the Advanced owner records that it remains the subject of post-event research, and the first-party parliamentary answer describes the flood and the response without asserting a mechanism. ❗ No reconstruction is offered here.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Salient features of world's physical geography" (Mains General Studies Paper I, direct ownership). ⚠️ The Himalayan hazard material serves the *changes in critical geographical features and the effects of such changes* demand family.
- **Verified linkage:** ❗ **this topic owns no General Studies Paper III demand in any year from 2018 to 2026.** 🔁 Every glacier, landslide and outburst demand in the General Studies Paper III ledgers is routed to the **disaster-management owner**, and the four such demands are listed in the hand-off box above and again in the final previous-year section. ⚠️ This lesson supplies the second effect pathway of the owned 2020 demand carried at Lesson 15.
- **Probable framing:** as the hazard-chain paragraph in a cryosphere answer; as a *"discuss glacial lake outburst floods"* demand, for which the Core owner's directive note requires the **dam-weakness-plus-trigger chain and the exposure half of the risk**; or as the physical half of a disaster question whose institutional half is routed.
- **Traps:** treating an outburst as ordinary flooding; merging triggers and breach modes; attributing an event to warming; asserting that earthquakes are the cause; reading area growth as a forecast; quoting a lake area, breach volume or casualty figure not carried by a first-party document; drifting into response doctrine.
- **Answer use:** the reusable sentence is *"An outburst flood is a dam failure rather than a rainfall event: retreat creates a lake behind a ridge of unconsolidated, unsorted till that often contains buried ice, and a trigger — an avalanche-generated displacement wave, extreme rainfall, piping, an earthquake or an upstream failure — converts a weak dam into a sediment-charged wave; but the losses are the product of that hazard with the roads, towns and hydropower built on the valley floor."*

Revision notes:

- 🔁 ⚠️ This lesson is **Lesson 8's moraine material and Lesson 9's buried-ice mechanism applied to a dam**. The weakness is a consequence of **how the material was deposited**.
- ⚠️ **The chain:** retreat → meltwater ponds behind moraine or in the vacated basin → **proglacial or moraine-dammed lake grows** → **the dam is unconsolidated debris, often with buried ice, and inherently weak** → **trigger** → **breach** → **high-energy, sediment-charged flood wave** down a steep valley.
- ⚠️ **Triggers:** rock or **ice avalanche** into the lake · **extreme rainfall** · **seepage and piping** · **earthquake** · **upstream lake failure in cascade**.
- ❗ **Breach modes are NOT triggers: overtopping · piping and internal erosion · mechanical collapse** (classically from **buried ice melting out**). **Freeboard** governs the first.
- ❗ ⚠️ A **displacement wave** can overtop a dam **with no change in lake volume** — so **area monitoring is not a safety assessment**.
- ⚠️ **Three properties that distinguish an outburst from ordinary flooding:** sudden onset with **no meteorological precursor** · **sediment-charged**, behaving partly as a debris flow · **valley-confined**, so depth and velocity persist far downstream.
- ❗ ❌ **Refuse single-cause attribution.** The signal is retreat, but the response is **heterogeneous**; and **warming does not by itself establish an event-specific cause**. ✅ *"The exact trigger chain remains a subject of post-event research."* ✅ Outbursts are **not** caused only by earthquakes.
- ❗ ✅ **Central Water Commission, August 2025, verbatim:** *"it is not feasible to make a reliable prediction of a specific occurrence on the basis of our existing knowledge"*; and therefore *"there is an urgent need to monitor a careful selection of prioritized lakes on a regular basis."*
- 📰 ✅ **South Lhonak (Lok Sabha Starred Q 68, 7 December 2023):** water spread **100-160 ha** over June-October monitoring, **2011-2023**; maxima **169 ha (Sept 2022)** and **171 ha (Sept 2023)**. ❗ **These figures and no others.**
- 📰 ✅ A **C-DAC water-level sensor installed in 2016 was damaged and collapsed after 6 months**; *"at present, there is no operational early warning system installed at South Lhonak Lake."* ✅ An **NDMA pilot** for South Lhonak and **Shako Cho** followed, with the **Swiss Development Corporation**.
- 📰 ✅ **The flash flood of 3 October 2023**: **Teesta-III dam (Sikkim Urja Limited) washed out**; sirens sounded, **Chungthang** informed, **about 250 people** evacuated from dam-site colonies; **NDRF deployed 6 teams in Sikkim and 5 in West Bengal**, 4 October to 1 November 2023; **46 human casualties including 15 army personnel** reported by the State Government.
- 📰 ✅ **Exposure, quantified (8 August 2024):** design floods of vulnerable dams under review for **probable maximum flood or standard project flood plus outburst flood**; **outburst studies mandatory for all new dams with glacial lakes in their catchments**; the **Central Electricity Authority identified 47 dams — 38 commissioned, 9 under construction** — as likely to be affected, with **studies completed for 31 projects**.
- ⚠️ **Cascading risk:** an outburst may be **initiated by a mass movement**, and may trigger further failures downstream. ✅ The Wadia Institute's research covers **Kedarnath 2013, Chamoli 2021 and South Lhonak 2023**.
- 🔁 **Hand-off:** response doctrine, deployment, standard operating procedures, early-warning governance, landslide and avalanche doctrine and infrastructure-resilience policy → **disaster-management owner**, which owns **four** verified demands. 🔁 Avalanche and rockfall mechanics → weathering and mass-movement owner; seismic hazard → earthquake owner.
- ❗ **Hazard ≠ risk.** Risk = hazard × exposure × vulnerability, with warning and response. ⚠️ The Core owner: the hazard has grown through **both** cryospheric change **and** increased exposure.

### Concept check

**Question:** After an outburst flood destroys a hydropower project and several bridges in a Himalayan valley, a commentator writes that climate change caused the disaster and that better glacier monitoring would have prevented it. Assess both claims, using the governing causal discipline, and state what can be said with confidence.

**Model answer:** **Neither claim survives examination, though each contains a true element.** On causation, the regional signal is genuine: Himalayan glaciers are losing mass, retreat is creating and enlarging moraine-dammed lakes, and that increases the population of potential sources. But the governing causal control is explicit that warming can alter glacier and lake conditions and yet **does not by itself establish an event-specific cause**; dam material, freeboard, slope instability above the lake, displacement waves, inflow, valley geometry, exposure, warning and infrastructure vulnerability must each be tested. The Advanced owner records of the best-documented recent Indian event that *the exact trigger chain remains a subject of post-event research*, and the official record also shows the response is **heterogeneous** — one sector's mean length change is indistinguishable from zero and an official annexure records a glacier as having **surged**. On prevention, monitoring would not by itself have prevented the loss. The Central Water Commission's own conclusion is that *it is not feasible to make a reliable prediction of a specific occurrence on the basis of our existing knowledge*; a displacement wave can overtop a dam with no change in lake volume, so an area series may give no precursor at all, and the best-monitored Indian case had an area within its long-run range in the season before failure. What can be said with confidence is this: regional cryospheric change has increased the number and size of moraine-dammed lakes; the dams are unconsolidated, unsorted and often contain buried ice, so they are inherently weak; the losses are the product of that hazard with exposure, since roads, settlements and run-of-river hydropower occupy the narrow valley floors; and the actionable responses are prioritised monitoring, dam-condition and exposure-based ranking, revised design standards and siting — not prediction.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat a stable or slowly growing lake area as evidence of safety. Area measures the reservoir, not the dam, and the failure modes that matter most — a displacement wave, piping through a loose dam, or collapse as buried ice melts out — can all operate without any prior change in the water surface that a satellite would record.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "A glacial lake outburst flood is a disaster of two independent causes." Discuss. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The formulation is accurate. The first cause is cryospheric. A negative mass balance withdraws the snout, meltwater ponds in the vacated basin or behind the terminal moraine, and a lake grows behind a dam of unconsolidated, unsorted till, never compacted and often containing buried ice. A trigger — an avalanche-generated displacement wave, extreme rainfall, seepage and piping, an earthquake, or an upstream failure in cascade — converts that weak dam into a sudden, sediment-charged flood wave confined by a steep valley. The second cause is human and independent: roads, settlements and run-of-river hydropower occupy the narrow valley floors, the only flat ground available. The Central Electricity Authority has identified 47 dams, 38 commissioned and 9 under construction, as likely to be affected by outburst floods from glacial lakes in Indian territory. Neither cause alone produces a disaster; the qualification is that attributing a particular event to warming is unsupported.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for the hazard chain given in order with the dam material specified; 3 for at least three triggers and one breach mode distinguished from them; 1 for the exposure lane identified as an independent cause; 2 for a dated first-party exposure instance; 1 for the hazard-against-risk identity and the refusal of event-specific attribution. Deduct 2 for describing the flood as ordinary flooding and 2 for asserting that warming caused a named event.
